GCF/B.30 /02/Add. 09 
Page 105 
 
 
2.1 Programme  background  
2.1.1.  Programme context  
3. Cooling is critical for health  and well -being , livelihood s and the environment . Cooling is 
an essential part of vaccines and food systems  to keep vaccines safe  and food fresh,  and is 
central to industrial and transport in frastructure . Cooling has also  become a mainstay for 
maintaining “comfort” in buildings for both work and leisure to ensure people  have comfortable 
buildings to live and work in.  Population growth, including a growing middle -class population , 
and chang es in lifestyles, rapid urbanization and rising ambient temperatures are  leading to an 
increase in cooling demand year on year. Therefore,  an estimated 3.6 billion cooling appliances 
currently in use is projected to increase to 9.5 billion by 2050, according t o Dreyfus et al.  2020, 
and the demand would be at around 14 billion if all people in need of cooling were to be catered 
for, as oppose d to only  those  who could afford it.  
4. According to SEforALL (2020) , over 1 billion people in developing countries are already 
facing an immediate risk from lack of access to cooling , of whom 699 million are slum dwellers 
living in hotter -climate urban areas where electricity services do not exist, are intermittent or 
are too expensive; and about 318 million are people liv ing in poor rural areas without access to 
cold chains for storage and preservation of food and medicine.  Therefore, cooling is not a luxury 
but a necessity for sustainable development . 
5. This new warming wor ld poses numerous climate challenges related to both mitigation 
and adaptation . First ly, the increase in cooling demand leads to high levels  of greenhouse gas 
(GHG)  emissions,  as the primary energy comes from carbon -intensive sources . Second ly, most 
of the  refrigerant s in use today for cooling are fluorinated gases (F -gases) – 
hydrochlorofluorocarbons (HCFCs)  or hydrofluorocarbons (HFCs)  – these F -gases have a very 
high global warming potential (GWP)  ranging from 92 to 14 ,000 , and if leaked or vented will 
cause substantial emission s. Thirdly , the Intergovernmental Panel on Climate Change  in 2019 
reported that at 1.5°C of warming  – a threshold that could be reached as early as 2030 – 2.3 
billion people could be both exposed and vulnerable to heatwave events , which can cause 
illnesses including heat cramps, fainting, heat exhaustion , heat stroke and even death. Heat 
stress will also impact productivity . The International Labour Organization  (2019) estimates the 
productivity loss due to heat by 2030 to amount to the equivalent of 80 million full -time jobs.  
2.1.2.  Program me description  
6. As mentioned above , cooling has become a critical part of daily lives, be it in comfort, 
supply chain management, tr ansport or industrial process. However, cooling has always  been  a 
“part” of the overall sector and never a stand -alone initiative. Therefore, clear focus on cooling 
has not been given. For example, cold chains are part of the cooling action , but it is embo died 
within the overall a griculture  value chain.  
7. The Cooling Facility will be  one of the world’s first cooling -focused facilit ies with the  aim 
of provid ing key solutions to cooling from multiple angles,  including  regulation and policy, 
technical assistance , and most importantly financing  to address multiple barriers inhibiting the 
broader adoption of clean cooling solutions , which is energy efficient and with l ow-GWP 
refrigerant.  
8. Key barriers to be addressed by the Cooling Facility  are the following:  
(a) Lack o f regulatory/policy framework for sustainable cooling system s along with limited 
institutional capacity to design, implement, monitor, and enforce cooling standards ; 
(b) Lack of enforceable standards like Minimum Energy Performance Standards (MEPS)  and 
Standar ds and Labels (S&L) , which target a specific sector – in this instance cooling.


---
*Page 106*
---

 
GCF/B.30 /02/Add. 09 
Page 106 
 
 
MEPS and S&L can be further operationalized into government fiscal and monetary 
policy as indirect incentives and lead to better enforcement ; 
(c) Lack of awareness among users of c ooling equipment . Few users are fully aware of its 
emissions impact, energy consumption, maintenance requirements and operating costs 
over time. The driving incentive for users is price and not efficiency ; this locks in people 
into inefficient equipment fo r the life duration of that inefficient equipment ;   
(d) Lack of skills:  in many countries, there are not  enough  sufficiently trained 
engineers/technicians to install and maintain cooling equipment. Even t he most efficient 
equipment , if not installed and  maintained correctly,  will not perform in the manner it 
was designed  to;  
(e) Lack of data and credible forecasts for the future scaling up  of cooling needs including 
costs, benefits and climate impact. Quality baseline data about equipment stocks, 
equipment sales and refrigerant inventories in many countries are either not present or 
not organized for ease of access ; and  
(f) In addition, m arket barriers include  the following:  
(i) Lack of access to finance for cooling investment (greenfield and retrofit) ; 
(ii) Lack of innovation in business models ; most countries still have high collateral 
requirement and do  not offer other services ;  
(iii) Lack of technology choice ; generally most markets are dominated by few 
suppliers ; 
(iv) Lack  of bargaining p ower , as most projects are small in size and thus do  not have 
the leverage to bargain with financiers for better terms ; and  
(v) Lack of pipeline ; consumption patterns and consumer behavio ur ha ve to 
demand efficient services as opposed to accepting the lowest p rice principle . 
2.1.3.  Cooling Facility d esign  
9. The wide range of cooling needs and priorities implies an equally diverse set of 
challenges and solutions as outlined  above. Therefore, the Cooling Facility is uniquely designed 
based on the principle of “no one -size -fits-all solution or approach”. Given the nascent and still 
early stage focus on cooling , globally , to address both development and climate  change  
purposes, the Cooling Facility seeks to cater to different contexts – countries’ geographic  and 
socio -econom ic contexts,  and efforts and priorities to respond to the crisis caused by the COVID -
19 pandemic crisis (e.g. vaccination supply chain).  
10. In this context, the Cooling Facility will support activities that address and help remove 
regulatory/policy -related, institutional, market and behavioural barriers to the development of 
sustainable cooling investments by: 
(a) Providing technical assistance and building the capacity of state and non -state actors 
(such as commercial banks, private investors and technical compa nies, and end user 
beneficiaries) ; 
(b) Supporting the design of sustainable implementation mechanisms and business models;  
(c) Increasing end -user awareness of the benefits of low -carbon cooling solutions ;  
(d) Facilitating access to affordable sources of financing; and  
(e) Provid ing credit lines to financial intermediaries.   
11. In turn, these activities will : (1) strengthen the capacity of key stakeholders ; (2) 
stimulate the demand for climate -friendly cooling solutions ; (3) foster the penetration of


---
*Page 107*
---

 
GCF/B.30 /02/Add. 09 
Page 107 
 
 
climate -friendly equipment and infrastructure ; and (4) mobilize capital from public and private 
sources.  
12. These interventions will strengthen institutional and regulatory systems, improve 
access to cooling, improve the adaptive capacity of end -user beneficiaries to respond to rising 
temperatures, as well as increase energy efficiency of cooling buildings and cooling 
infrastructure. Ultimately, the climate change resilience of built infrastructure will be enhanced , 
while the  carbon intensity of buildings, cold chains and cooling appliances will decrease.  
13. In fact, the diversity of the selected countries targeted by the Facility is expected to 
provide pioneering examples for mitigation and adaptation activities related to cooling in 
different contexts. Aside from different cooling sub -sectors , many issues are country -specific, 
and thus the Cooling Facility aims to support different approaches and generate lessons that can 
then be adapted and scaled  up more broadly to enhance ac cess to affordable cooling , while at 
the same time kick start a transformation towards more sustainable cooling, particularly by 
offering examples to be emulated and ad apted in countries beyond the scope of the Facility.   
14. Where growing cooling demand is creating pressures on a country’s energy systems and 
upward pressures on GHG emissions, the priority is to support space cooling, in terms of both 
passive measures (e.g. building design and high -efficiency thermal envelopes) and energy -
efficient cooling equ ipment and systems (this is the case for Bangladesh, North Macedonia, 
Panama and Sri Lanka). In agriculture -dominant economies where there is a need to expand 
access to cooling into rural and off -grid areas, the priority is to support an efficient and climate -
friendly cold chain (e.g. through the Facility’s proposed support to Malawi and Kenya) . 
15. In hot climate countries, ensuring health infrastructure is adequate for patients and 
storage of pharmaceuticals is a priority, because pharmaceuticals expire and b ecome ineffective 
more quickly in hot climatic conditions. Efficient and solar -powered cold chain and health 
facilities can expand quality health services and ensure safe storage of vaccines while making them more resilient to warmer temperatures, as well as keeping their carbon footprint low (e.g. 
El Salvador, Sao Tome and Principe, and Somalia) . 
16. There is an urgent need to cut emissions and meet increasing demand for future cooling 
sustainably. The Kigali Amendment to the Montreal Protocol, which has already been ratified by 
over 100 countries, and regional frameworks such as the E uropean Union  F-Gas Regulation, 
demonstrate how refrigerant emissions can be reduced, yet  further refrigerant phase -downs 
and phase -outs are needed. Meeting future cooling needs s ustainably can reduce the costs of 
renewable energy build -out by up to USD 3.5 trillion by 2030 and accelerate the net -zero 
transition by up to eight years. Hence,  the Cooling Facility will pioneer the way forward to 
meeting these targets.  
2.1.4.  Integrated and h olistic approach 
17. The scale of the cooling challenge and the window of opportunity to get on a path to 
affordable, efficient  and clean cooling require  an approach that integrates the development and 
strengthening of an enabling environment.  Doing this invol ves technical assistance,  capacity -
building,  and concessional  financing  to mainstream sustainable cooling and establish 
sustainable implementation and business models.  Moreover, in line with the Facility’s goals to 
support access to affordable, efficient  and clean cooling in a warming world, the Facility will 
integrate efficiency , starting with passive measures,  and low -carbon considerations and adopt a 
holistic approach to sustainable cooling consisting of  one or more of  the following elements:   
(a) Minimize : reduce and avoid cooling loads , for example, with passive building design, 
increase of thermal efficiency of buildings and cold storage warehouses/facilities,  and 
nature -based solutions such as green roofs ;


---
*Page 108*
---

 
GCF/B.30 /02/Add. 09 
Page 108 
 
 
(b) Shift to cooling  solutions with the lowest GH G emissions, for example solar cooling, 
climate -friendly (low GWP) refrigerant -based cooling systems,  
or centralized  cooling  solutions  where the right enabling conditions are in place;   
(c) Improve efficiency : use energy -efficient technologies to achieve cooli ng with less energy 
(and less GHG emissions), for example, super -efficient appliances and equipment and 
demand management to reduce peak demand and the need for new generating power 
capacity ;    
(d) Optimize : control cooling loads, for example, with smart sensing and behavio ural change 
approaches ; and 
(e) Protect the most vulnerable  in urban and rural areas from extreme heat and the 
consequences of unreliable or insufficient medical and agriculture cold chains . 
18. The total project budget is USD 879.84 million, of which USD 157 million are requested 
from GCF, of which USD 125 million is the form of loans, and USD 32 million is in the form of grants. The environmental and social safeguards categorization of this proposal is assessed to 
be I-2. 
2.2 Component -by-component analysis  
Component 1: Policy, regulatory and enabling environment support (total cost: USD 91.6 million; 
GCF cost: USD 15.7 million)  
19. This component will support activities that aim to strengthen institutional, policy and 
regulatory frameworks, raise awareness and stimulate behaviour al changes, as well as build the 
capacity of key stakeholders to plan, finance and implement sustainable cooling investments, 
and to plan for responses to increased heatwaves and other extreme events. Enabling activities 
will include cooling -specific inputs (related to both climate mitigation and adaptation) into the 
country’s nationally determined contributions planning process under the Paris Agreement such as Cooling Action Plans, as well as inputs on national policies and regulations that 
encourage sustai nable cooling. It will also support activities linked to the Facility’s Gender 
Action Plan.  
20. Activities relevant to the eligible cooling areas supported under Component 1 may 
include  the following:  
(a) Capacity -building of key stakeholders , for example, on tech nologies , business models,  
cooling project appraisal, implementation and evaluation;  
(b) Technical assistance for enabling plans, strategies, policies, regulations and standards;  
(c) Designing sustainable market mechanisms and business models; and  
(d) Communications  and awareness campaigns .  
Component 2: Financing for cooling investments (total cost: USD 769 million; GCF cost: USD 191.3 
million)  
21. In order to promote and accelerate the uptake of investments in innovative and climate -
friendly cooling technologies and sy stems, including in markets with low penetration and 
unmet cooling demand, the Cooling Facility aims to support increased access and deployment of 
sustainable cooling solutions performing better than current market practice and at the same 
time ensure that  the level of ambition is appropriate and relevant to each market (i.e. 
affordable) to expand access to – and deployment of -  more climate -friendly cooling at scale and 
avoid promoting a prohibitively expensive market niche, which would hinder the goals of  
scaling  up access and deployment.


---
*Page 109*
---

 
GCF/B.30 /02/Add. 09 
Page 109 
 
 
22. The Facility will provide  concessional  funds  to foster the adoption or scale  up the 
sustainable cooling technologies, appliances’  deployment through financing  of climate -friendly 
cooling  investment  and credit lines to fin ancial intermediaries.  
23. The financial instruments will be calibrated to what is needed to make the cooling 
project viable and to overcome barriers to implementation, including addressing the 
incremental cost associated with the adoption of efficient coolin g solutions or serve remote off -
grid areas, which include higher upfront capital costs compared to baseline options, and market 
entry barriers arising from the underrepresentation of efficient  and clean cooling solutions in 
many developing country regions and sectors. The deployment of these funds will be tailored to the needs of the projects, through various financing modalities, for example, public financing  to 
sovereign or sub -sovereign entities  credit lines to commercial banks, loans or subsidies to 
households, municipalities and small, medium and large enterprises.    
24. This component will focus on different financing and implementation mechanisms and 
models suitable for different cooling sectors and different countries , which  may be deployed by 
different projects of the Facility based on specific country and sector conditions.  Examples of 
these financing and implementation mechanisms and models  are as below.  
2.2.1.  Public  procurement and contracting  for cooling  infrastructure service 
delivery and technical assist ance  
25. GCF funds  would be used  to support investments through  a traditional public 
procurement and contracting model. Interventions that promote the deployment of a public 
good (for example, refrigeration and cold chain for vaccines and medicines  such as in El 
Salvador,  Sao Tome  and Principe and Somalia). This model includes the following  activities:  
(a) Prepare the technical specifications and terms of reference of the cooling infrastructure 
or equipment to  procure;  
(b) Launch the procurement process to invite bids from suitable contractors;  
(c) award the procurement contract;  
(d) Sign contractual agreements with the winning bidders;  
(e) Monitor the delivery of the goods and services to ensure quality control using own 
resources or through an owner’s engineer procured by the project ; and 
(f) Remunerate the contractor according to the agreed terms.  
Figure 1. Public  procurement and contracting  model


---
*Page 110*
---

 
GCF/B.30 /02/Add. 09 
Page 110 
 
 
2.2.2.  Credit lines   
26. A key barrier to  investing in  energy efficiency,  in general , and sustainable cooling , in 
particular , is the limited availability of  commercial financing  for these projects.  The Facility will 
support the  provision of credit lines,  for example  in Malawi,  to help financial 
institutions establish business lines for sustainable cooling  investments.  Credit lines  can be well 
suited  in less mature markets;  they  help  mitigate  the perceived  high financial risk  of energy 
efficient and clean cooling, which may be carried  out by  energy service  companies (ESCOs).    
27. An ESCO is an organization that provides  a range  of servi ces to energy users to design 
and implement cooling and energy efficiency measures. The services may also include providing 
or arranging financing. Such services are provided by the ESCO using a performance- based 
contract under which the payments from the energy user to the ESCO are contingent upon 
achieving certain pre -specified performance levels. This approach allows energy users to 
transfer much of a project’s  technical, construction, and performance risks to the ESCO. The 
concept of energy savings performance contracts (ESPCs) implemented by ESCOs has been 
recognized as a promising approach to overcome some of the most intractable market barriers.  
  
Figure 2. Implementation arrangement for  credit lines  
  
  
2.2.3.  Energy efficiency financing  facility   
  
28. Under this model, the  GCF  proceeds  are channell ed through the executing entity  
(EE).  The EE either  directly, or through an entity that it designates  (the project implementing 
entity (PIE) ), on-lends the funds  to sub-borrowers (for example,  priva te ESCOs , cooling as a 
service  operators, municipalities, or small - and medium -sized enterprises ) to undertake eligible 
cooling investments.


---
*Page 111*
---

 
GCF/B.30 /02/Add. 09 
Page 111 
 
 
Figure 3. Energy efficiency financing facility model  
  
  
29. The energy efficiency financing  facility  imple mentation  arrangement  involves two main 
variations, as presented below.    
30. (3-a) Public  super ESCO  model . In this variation of the energy efficiency financing facility 
model, the host country designates a public energy service company (the public super ESCO ). 
The public super ESCO would typically be a public entity established by the government to (a) 
support cooling and energy efficiency investments in the public sector (public buildings and 
sustainable cooling projects) and (b) strengthen private sector ES COs through demand 
aggregation, bulk procurement of equipment (therefore reducing the cost of equipment for 
ESCOs), standardization of ESPCs, monitoring and verification  templates and capacity -building, 
project development, and facilitation.  This variation of the  energy efficiency  financing  facility 
arrangement  is envisaged in Bangladesh.   
Figure 3 -a. Public  super ESCO implementation model


---
*Page 112*
---

 
GCF/B.30 /02/Add. 09 
Page 112 
 
 
31. (3-b) Energy efficiency revolving  facilities (EERF).  Under the EERF  model,  the 
PIE uses  revolving mechanisms to recover its investment and operating costs based on 
contracts such as energy service agreements (ESAs)  or also uses  more traditional revolving 
financing mechanisms such as debt financing and budget capture.5 5 Under an ESA, the facility 
finances and undertakes the investment on behalf of the beneficiary. In return, the beneficiary 
makes regular payments based on the energy savings generated by the investments to the EERF 
over an agreed period so that the EERF can recover the investment and operating costs. For the beneficiaries, the recurring payment (consisting of the reduced energy bill and the payment to 
the EE or  PIE, as applicable) is equal to or lower than their pre -investment baseline energy bill. 
For No rth Macedonia it is envisaged that GCF funds will be deployed through the  EERF model.  
 
Figure 3 -b Energy efficiency revolving facility (EERF)  model    
  
  
Component 3: Project management (total cost: USD 19.2 million; GCF cost: USD 0 million)  
32. This componen t will support the EEs and project implementing entities in the 
management, coordination and implementation of their project activities, in compliance with the contractual obligations that are included or referred to in the legal agreements entered into 
with the AE. There is no funding request from GCF for project management.  
III. Assessment  of performance  against investment criteria  
3.1 Impact potential                                                                      Scale: High  
33. The Cooling Facility has a mitigation impact potential of 16.24 million tonnes of carbon 
dioxide equivalent (MtCO 2eq) avoided for the lifetime of the project. A total of 4.22 million 
people will directly  of performance  benefit from the programme, in addition to 16.86 million 
indirec t beneficiaries. The Cooling Facility envisages enhancing the resilience of 18,057 physical 
assets , including buildings, agricultural and health cold chains.


---
*Page 113*
---

 
GCF/B.30 /02/Add. 09 
Page 113 
 
 
34. The Cooling Facility  is envisioned to increase access to cooling  that is needed in a 
warming world  and is especially critical to help build resilience in countries facing risks of 
extreme heat hazards, as well as help chart a sustainable path to low carbon development. As a 
cross -cutting facility the i mpact potentials are greatly heightene d as describe d below:    
(a) Adaptation  – The Cooling Facility will be engaged in countries facing a  high risk of 
extreme heat hazards : Bangladesh, El Salvador, Malawi and Somalia . The impact s of 
the Facility -supported sustainable cooling investment s are expected to 
reach  over  4 million  direct and  over  16 million  indirect  beneficiaries.  It will facilitate 
more than 16,000 climate -resilient  cooling assets ; and   
(b) Mitigation  – It is estimated that the Cooling Facility will lead to 
approximately  1 million  tonnes of avoided or reduced GHG emissions per year  across 
the sustainable cooling investments made in the nine  countries, and more than 14 
million ton nes of GHG reduced within the lifetime of the project. This amounts to nearly 
2 per cent  of the global co oling -related emission s, which is quite substantial  from a 
sector point of view.  
35. As a multi -sectoral facility , the impacts will be envisioned across these cooling sub -
sectors:  
(a) Kigali Amendment and refrigerants : The Facility will also  support  the goals and  
requirements of the Kigali Amendment to the Montreal Protocol by helping countries  in 
the transition  to more climate -friendly refrigerants  by catalysing  more energy -
efficient  cooling equipment (air conditioners and refrigeration)  in parallel with  HFC 
phas e-down.  The GHG benefits from this refrigerant transition supported by the 
Facility  have  been  estimated ex ante, in line with global estimates  indicating 
that GHG emissions from  the refrigerants represent about 20 per cent of cooling 
equipment’s  overall GH G emissions;   
(b) Space cooling: Space cooling projects will promote a) passive measures to provide 
comfort while avoiding the need for mechanical cooling, and b)  both  higher efficiency 
and lower  GWP refrigerants’  uses, thereby  shifting  the business -as-usual t rajectory of 
cooling  GHG impact  towards  sustainable  high efficiency/low GWP  cooling;  
(c) Refrigeration and cold chains : This will help catalyse a greening of the cold chain 
by improving  the efficiency of refrigeration, storage  and distribution  infrastructure  for 
agriculture and  health  using low GWP refrigerants.  This will be of particular importance 
to health  facilities and  cold chains to safely store vaccines and medicine, reducing 
emissions by 2.7  MtCO 2eq over the  lifespan of the programme;  
(d) Off-grid cooling  powered by renewable energy : Enabling the  expansion of  affordable 
cooling  in off -grid contexts  will help  reach some of the poorest communities and 
contribute to enhancing their resilience to the impact of climate change.  Indigenous and 
non- GHG emit ting renewable energy (solar)  will mostly power (126 MW)  these critical 
cooling services; and  
(e) Other impacts:  (1) climate -friendly vaccine cold chains in three countries (El Salvador, 
Sao Tome and Principe, and Somalia) will help them respond to the current  COVID -19 
pandemic,  increasing the capacity to store vaccines and  lower  the incidence of wasted 
vaccines; (2)  investments will help lower  the incidence of  heat -related illnesses, as well 
as mental stress; and (3) improved local air quality through the disp lacement/avoidance 
of diesel generators . 
3.2 Paradigm shift potential                                                    Scale: High


---
*Page 114*
---

 
GCF/B.30 /02/Add. 09 
Page 114 
 
 
36. There is huge  climate potential for cooling by maximizing energy efficiency 
(refrigeration and space cooling) while simultaneously phasing down HFCs contained in 
refrigerants ; the world can potentially avoid close to 1° C by 2100. The Cooling Facility aims to 
contribut e to unlocking the climate potential.   
37. The Cooling Facility envisions work ing across key cooling sub -sectors and with a 
range  of countries  from different parts of the developing world via a combination of targeted 
technical assistance and policy dialogue offered in conjunction with investment support for country -identified national priority sectors to:  
(a) Develop an enabling environment for cooling, via policy/regulations such as MEPS, S&L, 
National Clean Air Program s; 
(b) Build and generate a track record in the cooling sector ; 
(c) Amplify the potential to leverage experience gained and lessons learn ed;  
(d) Provide pioneering examples  that can be emulated  through  different  models and 
approaches that can be scaled up and  be fit -for-purpose i n more countries;  
(e) Allow governments to see how the policy  activities are delivered and delivering at sector 
level via investments ; and  
(f) Further stimulate  scaling  up the shift to sustainable cooling investments.    
38. The Cooling Facility will work with countri es to mainstream sustainable cooling into 
their development and climate plans, procurement strategies  and policy/regulatory 
frameworks and also help train and build the capacity of relevant stakeholders, while 
also  helping raise awareness of the availabili ty and benefits of sustainable cooling  measures and 
technologies.  
39. The Cooling Facility will in parallel also focus on different  implementing models:  a) 
credit lines that help  local banks develop  a business line for cooling  investments and  build their 
capa city to originate cooling  projects ; b) ESCOs  that  provide  financing as well as  services to 
design and implement  cooling and  energy efficiency  measures , to address both the financing 
and capacity barriers ; and  c) EERFs  collect energy  cost  savings  to recover  the capital 
expenditure for  cooling  investments  so that the funds can be  re-invested  and 
the EERF  can become a sustainable entity.  
40. The combinations of policy and investment via the Cooling F acility will : (a) set up 
revolving  financing  that allow s financi ng to continue after the end of the  projects; ( b) enabl e 
private sector entities to engage and invest in  project activities in the longer term ; as well as ( c) 
send strong market signals for shifts towards sustainable cooling through public procurement , 
thus exemplifying the financial sustainability and scale -up potential within the cooling sector.  
3.3 Sustainable development potential                                        Scale : High  
41. The programme has a high potential to generate environmental and health benefits, 
including reduction in air pollution, increase in access to resources and cold chains for vaccines and health infrastructure development.  
42. The Cooling Facility will provide b enefits over multiple Sustainable Development Goals 
(SDGs ). For instance:  
(a) Decent Work and Economic Growth (SDG 8) : Higher temperatures and lack of cooling 
negatively impact performance labo ur productivity. It is estimated that by 2050, work 
hours lost due to heat may be as high as 12 per  cent in the worst affected regions of 
South Asia and West Africa – representing 6 per  cent of annual GDP ;


---
*Page 115*
---

 
GCF/B.30 /02/Add. 09 
Page 115 
 
 
(b) Sustainable Cities and Communities (SDG 11) : In urban environments, average 
temperatures have been increasing at twic e the rate of global change, largely due to the 
Urban Heat Island effect. Annual mean air temperatures of cities can be 1 –3 °C higher 
than their surroundings (at night, the temperature difference can be as high as 12  °C). 
The need to reduce excess urban he at is one of the key resilience and sustainability 
challenges of the 21st century ; 
(c) Zero Hunger (SDG 2) : In developing countries around 40 per  cent of food produced 
(amounting to more than 250 million ton nes), including one  in four  fish caught, is lost 
betw een harvest and market ; 
(d) Good Health and W ell-being (SDG 3) : High temperatures increase the risk of heatstroke, 
breathing issues, heart attacks, asthma  and kidney problems. Unreliable medical cold 
chains put at risk the effectiveness of vaccines and other medical supplies;    
(e) Gender Equality (SDG 5) : According to SEforALL 2019, a gender equality risk is evident 
with regard to cooling but more data are needed. Women and girls are often 
disproportionately impacted by a lack of access to  sustainable cooling. The  Facility  will 
be able address gender gaps across entrepreneurs, employees and at the consumer 
level; and  
(f) Affordable and Clean Energy (SDG 7)  and Climate Action (SDG 13).  
3.4 Needs of the recipient                                                               Scale: High 
43. The Cooling Facility will enable the target countries to meet their cooling needs in a 
more climate -friendly way and contribute to lower GHG emissions  by: ( a) strengthening their 
institutions and implementation capacity to adapt to  rising temperatures; and ( b) addressing 
the barriers for clean cooling. The bottom -up approach taken by the Facility will provide 
favo urable conditions for local recipients to addre ss the issue of  sustainable cooling solutions 
by grounding mechanisms and  strategies  to each country’s  institutional and policy 
framework  and building on  countries’  priorities. The sub -sector and models are geared towards  
address ing the challenges and needs of the  local  recipients. The context for each country is as 
follows:  
(a) Bangladesh : The country faces serious energy security challenges and severe heat 
stress, equally across its population across different income level s. Integrating  energy 
efficiency a nd passive building design features and energy efficient cooling at scale now 
is key for building a more sustainable infrastructure at lower costs and lower pressures 
on the electricity grid and also in  line with the Environment Conservation Act and the 
Bang ladesh Energy Efficiency  and Conservation Master Plan ; 
(b) El Salvador : To ensure an effective COVID- 19 response and generate long -lasting 
resilience and be prepared to handle future health cris es, the Government of El Salvador 
seeks to strengthen the overall national immunization and related health delivery 
systems.  This includes a variety of issues, including safe and appropriate cold chain and 
relates services ;  
(c) Kenya : Demand for cooling is projected to grow significantly in the Nairobi and 
Mombasa markets, with a projected 1.8 million cumulative unit sales between 2020 and 
2030 for off -grid households alone. There are an estimated 3.4 million off -grid 
households in Kenya that could realize direct net economic benefits from owning a refrigerator. Energy effic iency will help lower the projected energy consumption to 
meet Kenya’s cooling demand, and in doing so reduce utility bills for households and businesses, and reduce GHG emissions in line with the country’s climate mitigation 
goals ;


---
*Page 116*
---

 
GCF/B.30 /02/Add. 09 
Page 116 
 
 
(d) Malawi : A lack of acc ess to cooling,  in particular more  sustainable cooling solutions with 
lower operational costs, is hindering economic opportunities for micro, small and 
medium- sized enterprises  involved in a number of important sectors, such as dairy and 
horticulture (frui ts and vegetables).  Promoting investment opportunities in sustainable 
cooling investments, with a focus on women and the youth, will contribute to providing 
economic opportunities and strengthening the resilience to climate change of vulnerable 
and margina lized population groups, while also avoiding expensive and polluting energy 
consumption ;  
(e) North Macedonia : Buildings consume about 39 per  cent of the energy  in the country, 
and represent a major priority. The public sector has the greatest potential, with about 
35–40 per  cent savings, mostly in the health and education sectors. In the past , the focus 
has been on heating, but increases in summer temperatures in Europe have  increased 
the demand and need for cooling, especially in schools and h ospitals. Improving thermal 
renovations of public buildings will substantially reduce their future cooling loads, 
which will contribute to the country’s adaptation to the changing climate. In the context 
of the COVID -19 situation, the rehabilitation of health -care facilities, with adequate air 
conditioning systems, is expected to become a higher priorit y;  
(f) Panama : According to the National Energy Plan of Panama, cooling systems ’ 
consumption currently represent s about 40 per  cent of overall electricity consumption. 
The electricity sector is facing serious challenges in keeping up with rising demand 
coupled with increased air conditioning  penetration jumping from 8.9  per cent  in 2000 
to an estimated 66  per cent  by 2050 ; the need for efficient cooling system s is a priority 
for Panama;  
(g) Sao Tome and Principe : The Government was initially unable to quickly establish 
laboratory capacity to diagnose COVID -19. Notwithstanding the fact that the country is 
now better prepared to diagnose, isolate, treat and track COVID -19 contacts, a 
significant and more comprehensive strategy to strengthen  the public health systems is 
needed to continue with the mitigation measures to contain the spread of the disease  in 
the country and to design and imp lement the national COVID -19 immunization plan, 
which  includes a robust vaccine cold chain ;  
(h) Somalia : The global COVID -19 pandemic is a pressing problem in Somalia, exacerbated 
by an extremely weak primary health -care  system, inadequate surveillance and 
laboratory capacity, and limited hospital services. There are major gaps in cold -chain 
capacity, which prevents expanding the currently extremely low vaccine coverage and 
will make distribution of COVID -19 vaccines diff icult . Likewise pharmaceuticals  and 
medicines expire and become ineffective more quickly in the very hot climatic  
conditions. Moreover, without support, it is expected  that kerosene,  petrol  and diesel 
fuelled generator equipment will be moved to increase  cold storage capacity ; and   
(i) Sri Lanka : Commercial  buildings (including government buildings)  and industrial 
buildings contribute 60  per cent  of the electricity consumption in the country. The 
Sustainable Energy Authority has also has defined an ambitious De mand Side 
Management program me that includes chillers, fans, refrigerators, air conditioning and 
green buildings (including the potential of nature -based solutions such as green roofs 
and green walls). But the major barrier to meet the needs of the people for these 
technolog ies has been access to the right type of financing ; the Cooling F acility via the 
EER F with ESCO model could be a solution.  
3.5 Country ownership                                                      Scale: High


---
*Page 117*
---

 
GCF/B.30 /02/Add. 09 
Page 117 
 
 
44. The Cooling Facility is strategically aligned with the countries’ nationally determined 
contributions, the Montreal Protocol and its Kigali Amendments, and countries’ strategies. The 
relevant country engagements have been  discussed with relevant local  authorities and also 
presented to na tional designated authorities (NDAs),  which  have  issued  no-objection 
letters.  Also, stakeholder engagements have been undertaken and will be further expanded 
while developing country -specific intervention.  The following describes the government’s 
efforts a nd strategic vision for the energy efficiency sector.  
(a)  Bangladesh: The Government has prepared the Energy Efficiency  Conservation Master 
Plan 2015 -2030, which describes the  adoption and implementation of  energy 
efficiency  and conservation regulatory measur es. In addition, the  project will inform 
the Government’s efforts in developing  a national Cooling Action Plan ;   
(b) Kenya:  Under the Paris Agreement, Kenya has committed to reducing GHG emissions by 
30 per cent , or 142 M tCO 2eq, by 2030. The National Electrification Strategy presents a 
road  map for achieving universal access to electricity by 2022. Supporting off -grid 
cooling appliances and access under the Facility  will contribute to  both  of these  goals ;  
(c) Malawi:  Promoting  the use of energy -efficient technologies and designs is one of the 
strategies advocated for in the Government’s  priority areas under the Malawi Growth 
and Development Strategy (MDGS III). Since agriculture is one of the  major  contributors 
to GHG emission s, the Facility’s support of clean and sustainable cold chains in 
agriculture will contribute to the  achievement of the MDGS III goal;   
(d) North Macedonia:  The Facility will support achieving the Government’s energy saving 
commitments through establishing a financing mechanism and investing into efficient 
space cooling of buildings ;  
(e) Panama:  Panama has ratified the Kigali Amendment in 2017, which aims to phase 
down   HFC s and is currently implementing Stage II of its  HFC  Phase -out Management 
Plan under  the Montreal Protocol.  The Facility will support achieving the Government’s 
commitments through its support for efficient space cooling ;  
(f) El Salvador:  The Government is integrating climate -friendly investments in its 
national  program mes and its  NDC  includes  health as a sector affected by clima te 
change  and the promotion of a larger use renewable energy. This is aligned with  the 
Facility’s support for  climate -friendly health facilities  and cold chain s; 
(g) Sao Tome and Principe: The country’s NDC include promoting the greater use of 
renewable energy  and list s health as one of the sectors negatively impacted by climate 
change. The Facility will contribute to the Government’s NDC through supporting space 
cooling and cold chains in the health sector, including through the increased use of solar 
power ;  
(h) Somalia:  Somalia’s NDC related to scaling up the use of solar energy is consistent with 
the Facility’s aim to support for more climate -friendly health facilities and cold chain s, 
including through greater use of solar power ;  
(i) Sri Lanka: Demand Side Managem ent is identified as one of the seven mitigation actions 
of the energy sector NDC of Sri Lanka. The Energy Efficiency Building Code sets 
standards for  energy efficiency  in design and retrofits in buildings, at the same time 
providing methods for determinin g compliance. These efforts are aligned with the 
Facility’s support of space cooling and  energy efficiency measures in buildings.  
3.6 Efficiency and effectiveness                                                    Scale: High


---
*Page 118*
---

 
GCF/B.30 /02/Add. 09 
Page 118 
 
 
45. The project has a co-financing ratio of 1:4.6. The cost of mitigation is USD 54.2/tCO 2eq 
for the total project cost and USD 9.7/tCO 2eq in terms of GCF funding.  
46. The financial structure of the Facility balances the need to support low -carbon cooling 
investments, leverage add itional resources from private and commercial investors, and enhance 
the enabling environment for cooling. The Facilit y’s approach of matching the model to the 
context of the country and sector will provide the right solution at the right place instead of a 
“one size fits all” approach  which is never the case and generally not appreciated by countries.  
47. The economic analysis show s that the facility  yields a  financial and  economic internal 
rate of return  of -0.8 and 16.3  per cent,  respectively. The  economic v alue add ed of the  Facility 
is largely due to its contribution to GHG emission reduction  potential of the f unded  activities. 
Further, the economic value is  very sensitive to the estimated cost 
of recycling  (otherwise  conducting environmental remedial action s) for appliances that have 
exceeded their useful lives ; this would need to be tackled as part of component 1 .  
IV. Assessment of consistency with GCF safeguards and policies  
4.1 Environmental and social safeguards  
48. Programme background . The programme aims to suppo rt subprojects in the areas of 
space cooling and cool/green surfaces (including passive cooling strategies in the built 
environment, cooling equipment; building automation and controls; and solar and vegetative 
roofs and walls); and refrigeration, cold cha ins and logistics (including refrigeration, storage, 
and distribution activities, along with associated equipment and logistics). The programme 
consists of three components that focus on (1 ) supporting policy  and the regulatory and 
enabling environment to  strengthen institutional, policy and regulatory frameworks, among 
others; (2) provide financing for eligible cooling investments through financing modalities that 
will promote the adoption or sca ling-up of sustainable cooling technologies, appliances and 
business models; and (3 ) project management to support the EEs and PIEs perform their project 
management functions.  
49. Environmental and s ocial (E&S)  risk category and s afeguards instrument . The AE 
has assessed that the activities under the Cooling F acility will have potential for limited adverse 
E&S  risks and impacts which,  individually or cumulatively, are few, generally site -specific, 
largely reversible, and can be readily addressed through mitigation  measures. The supported 
activities are likely to have l ow/ moderate to s ubstantial risk as per the AE’s risk definition. The 
Secretariat confirms that the classification correspond s to the GCF’s E&S  risk classification of 
category B and that it is within t he risk accreditation of the AE. An e nvironmental and s ocial 
sustainability framework (ESSF) is prepared in accordance with the AE’s safeguards 
requirements. The programme’s environmental and social co -benefits include those relati ng to 
health s ince  the su pport for more sustainable space cooling will help lower the incidence of 
heat -related illnesses and mental stress. Improved local air quality through the 
displacement/avoidance of diesel generators b y replac ing them with  solar -powered cold chains 
is also expected. 
50. Compliance with GCF’s Environmental and Social Safeguards (ESS ) standards . The 
following discussions describe the programmes compliance with the GCF ESS standards:  
51. ESS1: Assessment and Management of E&S Risks and Impacts . The programme has 
submitted an ESSF that provides a description of how the E&S risks and  impacts will be 
assessed, managed, and supervised at both the framework level and the operational level. The 
ESSF describes the necessary instruments and information  related to environmental and social 
impact assessment or audit and management at the programme level. The ESSF also outlines the


---
*Page 119*
---

 
GCF/B.30 /02/Add. 09 
Page 119 
 
 
impacts of the proposed programme as well as possible mitigation measures to address these 
impacts. The potential E&S risks an d impacts that will be considered in the conduct of due 
diligence for the sub -projects include those related to community health and safety; climate 
change and other transboundary or global risks and impacts; and threat s to the protection, 
conservation, ma intenance and restoration of natural habitats and biodiversity and those 
related to ecosystem services and the use of living natural resources. P otential social risks and 
impacts, include threats to human security through the escalation of personal, commun al or 
inter -state conflict, crime or violence; risks that impacts will fall disproportionately on 
disadvantaged or vulnerable individuals or groups; prejudice or discrimination toward s 
individuals or groups in providing access to development resources and benefits; negative 
economic and social impacts relating to the involuntary land acquisition or restrictions on land 
use or  regarding land and natural resources; impacts in relation to occupational health and 
safety of workers; and risks to cultural herita ge. The programme is expected to channel 
financing through financial intermediaries, which  will be required to establish and maintain an 
environment and s ocial management system to identify, assess, manage, and monitor the E&S  
risks and impacts of the subp rojects.  
52. ESS2: Labour and working conditions . Construction and implementation of the 
subprojects will involve hiring workers which will include either direct -hired or 
contracted/sub -contracted workers. Occupational health and safety plans will be prepared  as 
per the World Bank Group environment, health and safety guidelines and implement ers will 
have to comply with these  requirements including the preparation of a l abor management 
procedure. Since an influx of migrant labour  will also have potential impacts in relation to 
worker conditions, occupational health and safety, gender -based violence and potential conflict 
among the subproject workers as well as with the host local communities, these risks/impacts will be evaluated as part of the E&S risk assessments and will be managed in accordance with 
the labour management procedure  and other appropriate plans and procedures. These 
measures will likewise be included in the bidding and legal documents with contractors/sub -
contract ors. A dedicated grievance redress mechanism for subproject workers will also be 
established for each subproject.   
53. ESS3: Resource e fficiency and p ollution prevention.  While the interventions under 
the programme are generally expected to promote efficiency and resource conservation, 
construction of such facilities such as new buildings or retrofitting existing buildings is expected 
to generate typical construction impac ts such as dust and air pollution from excavations and 
vehicle exhaust; solid waste arising from construction and demolition including  the potential 
need to  handl e and dispos e of hazardous waste (e.g. asbestos and wastes from 
dismantling/disposal of old co oling equipment and refrigerant gases). These risks/impacts will 
be assessed in detail for each of the subproject s. However, given that these potential impacts are 
expected to be low to moderate and are reversible and localized in nature, appropriate 
mitig ation measures can be readily implemented in accordance with good international 
industry practice.  
54. ESS4: Community h ealth, safety and s ecurity . Constructing  facilities and 
implementing programme  activities will have potential to adversely affect the health , safety and 
security of the host communities. I mpacts may arise from noise and vibration;  dust emission ; 
and disposal of old parts and equipment which may contain  hazardous  waste. Traffic -related 
incidents as a result of vehicle transport in and out of co nstruction areas may also affect the 
nearby communities. Nevertheless, these risks and impacts which will be mainly on the communities’ safety , can be managed through proper implementation of adequate and 
appropriate mitigation measures as provided in the AE’s standards.  
55. ESS5: Land a cquisition and i nvoluntary resettlement . The programme may result to 
land acquisition for construction of new buildings or cold storage facilities and may have 
potential adverse environmental and social impacts associated with land acquisition or land


---
*Page 120*
---

 
GCF/B.30 /02/Add. 09 
Page 120 
 
 
conversion which will be managed consistent  with ESS5 requirements : that is,  avoid, minimize, 
reduce/mitigate and compensate at replacement cost lands that will be acquired based on due 
diligence and plans prepared in accordance with ESS5. An indicative resettlement policy 
framework and an outline of a resettlement action plan is provided in the ESSF to guide EEs in 
preparing specific resettlement action plan s (if required) in line with safeguard requirements.  
56. ESS6: Biodiversity c onservation and s ustainable management of l iving natural 
resources . Depending on the scale and location of the facilities that will be financed under the 
programme, there may be  potential loss of habitats and other adverse impacts to flora and 
fauna. Nevertheless, siting and careful selection of the area where these faciliti es will be 
constructed will dictate the extent of its impacts. To mitigate the impacts, care would be taken 
by the developers to ensure that the impacts on natural habitats, terrestrial and aquatic 
ecosystems and on sensitive species are well assessed and managed  through preparation of 
appropriate plans, as needed .  
57. GCF Indigenous Peoples Policy and ESS 7: Indigenous Peoples . The AE stipulates 
that the presence of indigenous people in the areas identified for the projects will be screened and confirmed by t he AE in consultation with the national authorities.  The AE outlines that , 
once a project is  confirmed, consistency with ESS7 objectives and requirements will be 
integrated into the design of the project and E&S  risk documents, including the development o f 
an indigenous peoples’ plan or other types of plans depending on the country and project 
context, and f ree, prior and i nformed consent in the circumstances specified in ESS7. For 
consistency with the GCF Indigenous Peoples Policy, the sub -project screeni ng will need to be 
inclusive for groups such as “Afro -descendent communities of South America and the 
Caribbean”, “local communities”, “scheduled tribes”, “hill tribes” and “tribal groups ”. 
58. ESS8: Cultural h eritage. The ES SF indicates that subprojects will be screened and 
assessed regarding  potential impacts on tangible and intangible cultural heritage. Site selection 
for each subproject will consider the application of ESS8 and will be further assessed under each subproject . Should there be possibility for presence and/or discovery of items of 
archaeological, paleontological, historical, cultural, artistic and religious value in the civil works 
sites during construction, a “ chance find procedure ” will be a part of the E&S ri sk instrument.  
59. Implementation arrangements . In accordance with the ESSF, the AE will manage the 
overall environmental and social performance of the Cooling F acility and that of the sub -
projects as well as monitor their performance in accordance with the re quirements of the legal 
agreements, including those in the e nvironmental and s ocial commitment plan that will be 
agreed between the AE and the borrowers. The AE will likewise provide implementation support regarding the environmental and social performance of the subprojects, which will 
include reviewing the borrower’s monitoring reports regarding their compliance , and will share  
such information  with the GCF through the a nnual performance reports. For each sub -project, a 
project management unit will be est ablished by the relevant EE which will be expected to 
include a team of experts including environmental specialist and social/gender specialist that 
will be tasked with ensuring adequate implementation of the management measures as well as 
the monitoring, evaluation and reporting during implementation.  
60. Stakeholder engagement and information disclosure . The programme has 
conducted preliminary stakeholder engagements in the targeted countries. These engagements 
included meetings and consultation workshops wit h representatives from implementing 
agencies at the national level  and regional level, local authorities of participating provinces, 
private investors, farmer representatives, and concerned communities. In addition, other 
interested parties include the med ia, local/international non- governmental organizations and 
development partners working in the same region. A detailed stakeholder engagement plan 
(SEP) will be developed for each of the subprojects to ensure transparency and meaningful 
consultation with t he affected peoples and interested parties. The SEP will include stakeholder 
identification, analysis, information dissemination, consultation and engagement thatwill be


---
*Page 121*
---

 
GCF/B.30 /02/Add. 09 
Page 121 
 
 
carried out during subproject design and implementation. The stakeholder identificatio n, 
analysis and engagement will be informed by the degree of potential risks and impacts of the 
subprojects and how these will affect the stakeholders.  
61. Grievance redress mechanism . The SEP for each of the proposed subproject under the 
facility  will include a g rievance redress mechanism that will be prepared and implemented by 
each implementing agency to receive and facilitate resolution of concerns and grievances from affected communities as well as broader stakeholders who may be affected or h ave interest in 
the subproject’s activities. The mechanism will be established to receive and record complaints 
as well as provide resolution and feedback on the status of the grievances to the complainants 
to ensure transparency and accountability. Communities and individuals may likewise submit 
complaints to the AE’s Grievance Redress Service that allows submission of grievances from 
affected communities and individuals. In addition, the GCF Indigenous Peoples P olicy states that 
the GCF indigenous peoples  focal point will be available for assistance at any stage, including 
before a claim has been made.  
4.2 The revised SEAH Policy  
62. The Revised Policy on the Prevention and Protection from Sexual Exploitation, Sexual 
Abuse, and Sexual Harassment (Revised SEAH Poli cy) includes a guiding principle related to 
Fund -related activities. It states that the “ GCF and its Covered Individuals shall not enter into 
future engagements with those who condone, encourage, participate in, or engage in SEAH in Fund -related activities ”.  As a result, the AE was requested to include the relevant covenant in 
the Term Sheet. As a form of this provision could not be agreed  in the Term Sheet, a secretariat 
condition has been added on this matter.  
4.3 Gender policy  
63. The AE provided a gender assessment and action plan with the funding proposal and 
therefore complies with the requirements of the GCF Gender Policy.  
64. The gender assessment presents the enabling environment in terms of applicable laws 
for non -discrimination and protection of women. Some  of the countries promote increased 
engagement of women in labour markets, including in the electricity, gas, steam and air 
conditioning supply sectors. Gender equality in terms of access to income, financial inclusion, 
access to health and education, repr esentation in political bodies is promoted in the countries. 
Although there are a number of laws enacted in all the countries to promote gender equality 
and prevent discrimination, women do not always have the same rights as men under some 
laws and they have lower access to resources compared with men. The assessment has fallen 
short of providing information on the existing national institutional frameworks that could support and ensure gender equality matters are integrated well in the programme’s benefici ary 
countries. The AE has not provided an in -depth analysis of the root causes for the inequalities 
and gender -based challenges faced by women, in particular from cultural, traditional and 
societal perspectives.  
65. The gender assessment is limited in that it  was compiled on the basis of  a review of 
literature available to the AE. The assessment does not include inputs from relevant 
stakeholders or has not benefited from a stakeholder engagement exercise that should have 
been undertaken during the preparation of the programme. The stakeholder engagement 
exercise to ensure relevance and effectiveness of the programme and project impacts on gender 
issues will be undertaken during the programme’s implementation.  
66. Information on gender -related issues on cooling is limited and descriptions of the 
preliminary identification of gender gaps do not articulate in detail the challenges faced by


---
*Page 122*
---

 
GCF/B.30 /02/Add. 09 
Page 122 
 
 
women and men in different countries or in the sectors that are likely to be supported by the 
Facility. With the limited information provided, understanding the probable impact on women’s 
participation and access to benefits of the programme as well as drivers of change is difficult. 
Despite that, some gender issues have been identified which may indicate impact, the level of 
engagem ent and benefit s to women , such as lack of access to jobs and skills development, access 
to finance and entrepreneurship. In addition, a list of possible interventions for women’s 
economic empowerment, such as access to services and leadership and decision -making , which 
may be implemented to address gender gaps. These actions could potentially be included in 
individual project -level gender action plans. The funding proposal further indicates that the 
programme will aim to enhance the knowledge base of gender issues associated with access to 
cooling and reduce gender gaps in cooling -related sectors . 
67. The assessment indicates that additional gender analyses will be undertaken at the 
project level, which will involve relevant stakeholders and which would have to address and 
include the many actions identified through the current gender assessment, address gaps and provide insight into the likely differentiated cooling needs and priorities based on defined 
responsibilities. Additionally, further work will need to be undertaken to ensure that various 
components of the programme mainstream gender and take gender issues as an important 
eligibility criterion in financing of projects.  
68. The programme will establish a grievance redress mechanism for each project , which 
will be made available for reporting and addressing grievances associated with sexual 
exploitation, abuse and harassment.  
69. The AE has provided a programme -level gender action plan that includes activities, 
indicators, some targets, responsibilities a nd timelines. As part of the budget for c omponent 1, 
financial resources have been set aside for undertaking further gender assessments al though 
there are no budget allocations for the implementation of the other activities in the action plan. 
Several targ ets have not been set and the setting of targets has been outlined as country 
dependent. The AE has proposed to collect baseline data during implementation which should be used to rationali ze and establish targets across all activities.  
70. Activities listed in the gender action plan are too general and set at a high level and fail 
to link and reflect the nature of the projects that have been identified and presented in a 
separate annex with the funding proposal, such as space cooling, cool surfaces, refrigeration, and cold chains in sectors such as health and agriculture. A few important gender co -benefits 
are outlined in the gender assessment and are reflected in the gender action plan , such as 
employment opportunities for women and access for women -owned bus inesses to cooling 
services, in an effort to ensure that direct benefits of the programme are reali zed by women. 
The activity on improving access for men and women to new cooling, refrigeration, cold chains, 
logistics and green solutions has not articulate d how this will be achieved and a corresponding 
indicator will track the number of both male and female beneficiaries, although no targets have 
been set. Activities also included in the programme -level gender action plan are related to 
participation in sta keholder engagement, capacity -building and awareness -raising events. Other 
activities in the gender action plan are actions that contribute to the development of further 
gender analyses and action plans at the project level,  which will be done by the AE th rough its 
EEs and do not warrant inclusion in the action plan as standalone activities (e.g.  identifying 
barriers that need to be addressed to promote the participation of women and prioriti ze actions 
that contribute to clos ing gender gaps ). The AE has  failed to include activities such as skills 
development in the cooling sector, entrepreneurship and access to finance/addressing 
affordability constraints, consumer engagement, addressing different needs of women and men 
for cooling technology products and se rvices in various sectors such as fisheries and agriculture, 
under component 2.


---
*Page 123*
---

 
GCF/B.30 /02/Add. 09 
Page 123 
 
 
71. Gender specialists are included in the project management unit for each project. Each 
specialist will be responsible for overseeing the implementation and reporting on activit ies in 
each respective action plan.  
72. The AE is advised to improve its gender mainstreaming approach to ensure that all 
components of the programme address the needs of women and men equitably. The AE is 
recommended to ensure that additional gender analyses at the project level for each of the nine 
countries integrate the needs and priorities of women and men, particularly closing the gender gaps in interventions that are dedicated to cooling. This should include opportunities to address 
them, in the priority  areas of intervention of the programme as would also be identified through 
stakeholder engagement. Stakeholder engagement should provide space for all relevant 
stakeholders and at all levels , including communities,  to engage and influence the design of th e 
gender action plans. Additionally, the AE is required to ensure that an action plan is prepared for each country not for just six when there are nine participating countries. The programme-
level gender action plan will need to be updated based on the fin dings of the aggregate, country 
and sector -based gender assessments and will need to reflect expected benefits and impacts. 
The updated programme -level gender action plan should include indicators, targets with 
baseline, timelines, responsibilities and all ocated budget s for their implementation. The GCF 
expects to monitor progress based on the updated programme -level gender action plan. The 
criteria for the selection of women -owned business es will need to be defined and activities to 
support women -owned bus inesses should be included in a revised gender action plan for the 
programme.  
4.4 Risks  
4.4.1.  Overall programme assessment : medium risk  
73. The funding proposal requests USD 125 million in sovereign loans and USD 32 million in 
grants. The programme targets support to the participating host countries to mainstream and 
bring to scale sustainable cooling solutions across key sectors. The host cou ntries of the 
programme are Bangladesh, El Salvador, Kenya, Malawi, North Macedonia, Panama, Sao Tome 
and Principe, Somalia and Sri Lanka. The programme consists of three components: (1) policy, 
regulatory and enabling environment support (USD15.7 million of GCF grant); (2) financing for 
cooling investments (USD141.3 million of GCF funds, of which  USD125 million in loan and USD 
16.3 million in grant); and (3) project management (no GCF funds requested). The AE envisions 
co-financing of USD 722.84 million o f which USD 50 million in guarantees, USD 109 million in 
grants and USD 563 million in loans. All co- financing amounts are indicative and co -financing 
tenor does not match GCF tenor. If the programme raises a lower amount of co -financing, the 
programme’s c limate impact may also get reduced while maintaining the same level of financing 
from GCF.  
4.4.2.  Accredited entity /executing entity capability to execute the current programme : 
medium risk 
74. The World Bank is the AE for the proposed programme. Given the structure of the 
programme, the broad eligibility criteria, and limited visibility at this stage regarding the end 
projects, the success of the programme depends to a great extent on the capabi lity of the AE. 
The AE has a strong institutional capacity and experience in executing similar programmes.  
75. For the purposes of the loans and grants, the respective host countries will be the EEs. 
They will either act through the relevant ministries or des ignate a PIE. The AE has at this stage 
not assessed the capacity of all EEs involved. It is advised that the AE shall complete such 
assessment before disbursement of the funds to such an entity.


---
*Page 124*
---

 
GCF/B.30 /02/Add. 09 
Page 124 
 
 
4.4.3.   Programme -specific execution risks : medium risk  
76. Demand risk : The impact achieved by the project will depend on the financing 
opportunities and early -stage projects maturing. Also, impact depends on continued utilization 
of GCF funds in such opportunities. Demand risk is partially mitigated by technical assistance 
under to programme to help beneficiaries and World Bank operational teams in the design of 
cooling informed projects including activities such as cooling market studies, sector gap analysis and strategies, knowledge sharing, stakeholder consultations, busin ess models identification. It 
is advised that the terms and conditions of the project require that GCF low concessional loans 
shall be invested in sub- projects for the duration of the programme.  
77. Sovereign credit risk : GCF will provide up to USD 125 millio n in sovereign loans under 
the programme. GCF will not benefit from the preferred credit status of the AE. The credit risk of 
the countries under the programme varies from lower medium grade (BBB by S&P, Panama) to 
substantial risks (CCC+ by S&P, Sri Lanka ). Several countries in the programme do not have a 
rating by a major rating agency (e.g. Somalia and Sao Tome and Principe). The AE has included in the term sheet that GCF loans will not enjoy the AEs preferred creditor status.   
78. Co-financing risk : Lower than indicative funding amounts provide by the AE and 
related entities could affect the impact of the programme. Co -financing presented in the funding 
proposal is indicative. The terms sheet contains no requirements on commitment of co -
financing before funded activity agreement (FAA) execution. A minimum co -financing ratio of 
1:2 is included in the term sheet on a per project basis, but this is below the co -financing ratio of 
1: 4.6 in section E.2 of the funding proposal. However, the AE notes that 54 per c ent of the co -
financing has already materialized. The World Bank and World Bank -managed trust funds have 
approved and signed the corresponding legal agreements with the recipient countries for six of the nine projects envisaged.  Achieving a lower amount of co-financing may result in reducing 
the climate impact of the project. It is recommended that a minimum co -financing ratio of 1:4 on 
a programme basis is included so that the efficiency of GCF funding remains as envisaged.  
79. Operational environment : Execut ion of the project will depend on the  technical 
capacity on the ground as well as political factors relating to affordability. The AE will develop a 
monitoring and evaluation framework  during the preparation phase to address technical 
capability risk and w ill take into account impacts on affordability of the interventions.  
80. Carbon credits : The termsheet includes no limitation on the sale of carbon credits. Sale 
of carbon credits could reduce the impact attributable to GCF. It is advised that limitations on 
the sale of carbon credits are included in the terms and conditions of the programme  
4.4.4.  Compliance risk : medium  risk  
81. Of the countries involved in this proposal, it is confirmed that Somalia is subject to 
United Nations Security Council (UNSC) Resolution 751 (1992) . However, the World Bank, as 
the AE, has  advised that it is not aware of any UNSC resolutions affecting Somalia that would 
impact on the proposed project being considered under the Cooling Facility. The World Bank 
acknowledges Resolution 751 (para. 14) insofar as it urges the international commu nity to 
facilitate humanitarian assistance to the Somali population, and it does not list/exclude any 
particular territory.  
82. In confirming that no individual or entity that is listed on any UNSC Sanctions list will be 
involved in any manner with the projec t or its activities, the World Bank referred to its 
obligations in this regard as stated in paragraph 4.05(a)(ii) the accreditation master agreement (AMA): 
(a) In preparing each Funding Proposal, the Accredited Entity shall:

Facility level. Such documents will be drafted during the project preparation phase, finalized 
during negotiations with host countries and publicly disclosed once the project is approved by 
the World Bank.

terrorism (AML/CFT) are very important and are comprehensively covered in the AMA and FAA 
template, which stipulate that the Wo rld Bank will apply its own policies and procedures on 
these matters. These policies and procedures require the World Bank to assess all the fiduciary 
risks and develop mitigation measures, which is undertaken during project preparation.

technology are not diverted or misused for prohibited practices are covered in the AMA and

through the FA A. For example, clause 11.01 of the FAA template provides, as follows : 
(a) The Accredited Entity’s AML/CFT procedures share the objective of the Fund’s AML/CFT Policy to ensure that the proceeds of any financing are used only for the purposes for which 
the fin ancing was granted, it being understood that the Accredited Entity shall be solely 
bound to apply its own AML/CFT procedures in accordance with the AMA and this 
Agreement.

cash, vouchers, commodities, or items of value to end user beneficiaries, whether directly or 
indirectly.

adversely affected by the project may submit complaints to existing project -level grievance 
redress mechanisms or the World Bank’s Grievance Redress Service.  
89. The Grievance Redress Service ensures that complaints received are promptly reviewed 
in order to address project -related concerns. Project -affected communities and individuals may 
submit their complaint to the World Bank’s Independent Inspection Panel, which determines 
whether harm occurred, or could occur, as a result of non -compliance with World Bank policies 
and procedures.

the risk appetite.  
94. Project viability and concessionality: The AE will differentiate the level of 
concessionality between the reimbursable instrument in each project by applying either low or 
high concessional loans. If the host country is in external debt distress,  it will receive GCF non -
reimbursable funds and will only receive co -financing exclusively on grant terms. A combination 
of high concessional loans and grants will be offered if (1) the host country is in high risk of debt distress; (2) the project does not generate any revenue or the reflows from the ultimate 
beneficiaries are not cost - reflective; and/or (3) the project targets vulnerable communities or 
groups that are more vulnerable than the overall population.  Any other projects will receive 
low conces sional loans.  
95. Within the above parameters, GCF shall rely on the AE to appropriately deploy the 
concessionality. It is advised that the terms and conditions include a covenant that require the 
AE to apply the minimum concessionality principle and to contr actually require of the AE that a 
substantial part of the concessionality is passed on to the end beneficiaries.

4.5 Fiduciary  
97. The World Bank will be the Accredited Entity for this programme. The AE will be 
responsible for ensuring that the Projects are developed and implemented by the relevant 
Executing Entities (EEs) and Project Implementing Entities ( PIEs) and approved by the A E in 
accordance with its policies and procedures and the relevant provision of the AMA and FAA, and 
selected in accordance with the Eligibility Criteria.  The AE will provide implementation support 
to the Projects including necessary monitoring, supervisio n, and quality assurance in 
accordance with its policies and procedures and the relevant provisions of the AMA and FAA . 
98. Each Project will be carried out through different Executing Entities (EEs) and PIEs as 
further described below .  EEs are as listed on FP.  For indicative purposes, Table 1 on the TS, 
sets out certain Executing Entities and PIEs that are anticipated to carry out part or all of Projects in specific Host Countries. For the avoidance of doubt, the AE will not act as an  EE for 
the Projects and the PIEs will not be included within the definition of EEs for this Programme. 
Although the PIEs are not included within the definition of EEs for this Programme, as they will 
carry out part or all of Projects in specific Host Coun tries, the AE will undertake due diligence on 
them in accordance with clause 10.02(a)(i) of the AMA as if they were included within the 
definition of EEs for this Programme and the outcome of such due diligence will be included in 
the respective Project ap praisal document.  
99. In respect of each Project for which a disbursement is requested, AE will deliver to the 
Fund a copy of the Accredited Entity’s Project appraisal document (PAD), containing 
information about the relevant Project. PAD will include the capacity assessment of the EEs . 
100. Each project financed through the Cooling Facility will be required to conduct financial 
management and procurement according to the World Bank policies and procedures. Each 
project supported by the Cooling Facility will follow the AE’s Procurement Framework and 
Regulations.  
101. Each project supported by the Facility is required to maintain financial management 
arrangements (including planning and budgeting, accounting, internal control, funds flow, 
financial reporting, and auditing arrangements of the borrower and entities responsible for 
project implementation) acceptable to the AE.  A financial management specialist is expected to 
be part of the team forming the PMU established by each relevant Executing Entity (or PIE as 
applicabl e) for its respective project.   
102. Moreover, for each project, the AE task team includes an accredited Financial 
Management (FM) specialist who provides support during the entire project cycle (preparation, 
supervision and implementation) in accordance with the AE’s operational policies and 
procedures (to work with partner countries to design financial management and disbursement 
arrangements and to supervise and support the performance of World Bank -finance 
operations). The team’s FM specialist closely monit ors the performance of the EE’s (or PIE as 
applicable) FM arrangements and their continued capacity to provide reasonable assurance that 
loan proceeds are being used for the purposes intended.
