4.6 Results monitoring and reporting  
104. As a cross- cuttin g initiative, the programme aims at generating both mitigation and 
adaptation benefits. On the mitigation side, the intervention is expected to result in overall GHG emission reductions of 16,240,201 tCO
2eq over a lifetime period of 27 years, as per the metrics 
of the GCF core indicator for mitigation based on GHG accounting methodology detailed under 
annexes 23 and 24 of the funding proposal.  
105. Regarding adaptation, the programme expects to benefit 4.22 million direct and 16.85 
million indirect beneficiarie s in the four countries at high risk of extreme heat hazard 
(Bangladesh, Malawi, El Salvador and Somalia)  through the implementation of space cooling, 
refrigeration and cold chain projects. The AE provided a robust methodology and rationale for 
defining b oth direct and indirect beneficiaries in annex 24 of the funding proposal.  
106. The theory of change adequately explains how change is understood to come about in 
the targeted countries. The goal statement is properly articulated, and the stated programme 
resul ts and outcomes are defined in a manner that is clearly supportive of meeting the ultimate 
project goal.  
107. The logical framework has been designed with relevant details, including the inclusion 
of solid means of verification and reporting on the appropriate core indicators for both 
mitigation and adaptation, as well as on the respective impact, outcome and programme -level 
indicators for the targeted results areas as per the GCF performance management framework and results measurement frameworks. At the design  stage the AE has not been able to provide 
credible baselines and targets for the three programme- level indicators for adaptation in 
section E.5, so the Secretariat has requested through a dedicate term sheet covenant, that those 
are updated every year in each annual performance report and finalized prior to the interim 
independent evaluation.  
108. The implementation timetable in Annex 5 of the Funding Proposal should be updated 
during the FAA negotiation when the exact timeline for submission of the initial APR  will be 
negotiated and agreed.    
109. The monitoring and evaluation plan (annex 11 of the funding proposal) has been found 
to follow the appropriate requirements because it includes the entire list of the programme logical framework indicators while ensuring alignment of the data/sources with established 
means of verification.  
110. The Secretariat considers, however, that the programme does not provide sufficient 
safeguards for the GCF mitigation impact and, therefore, has agreed to discuss further with the 
AE duri ng FAA negotiations the conditions on the potential issuance of carbon credits by the 
programme, while maintaining the integrity of the lifetime mitigation impact.

111. The AMA was signed with the AE on 13 November 2017, and it became effective on 21 
February 2019.

112. The AE has not provided a legal opinion/certificate confirming that it has obtained all 
internal approvals and that it has the capacity and authority to implement the programme. It is 
recommended that, prior to submission of the fundi ng proposal to the Board (a) the AE has 
obtained all its internal approvals and (b) the GCF has received a certificate or legal opinion 
from the AE in form and substance satisfactory to the GCF confirming that all final internal 
approvals by the AE have been obtained and that it has the authority and capacity to implement 
the programme.  
113. The proposed programme will be implemented in nine countries in which GCF is not 
provided with privileges and immunities: Bangladesh, El Salvador, Kenya, Malawi, North Mace donia, Panama, Sao Tome and Principe, Somalia and Sri Lanka. This means that, among 
other things, GCF is not protected against litigation or expropriation in this/these country(ies), 
which risks need to be further assessed.  
(a) Bangladesh : A draft agreement on  privileges and immunities is under negotiation. The 
Secretariat has liaised with the Ministry of Finance (Economic Relations Division) on the 
review of the draft agreement by the relevant involved ministries and agencies, as well 
as with the national desi gnated authority (NDA). In February 2020, the Secretariat 
resent the draft agreement, together with a background note on privileges and 
immunities, to the NDA; however, no formal response has been received thus far;  
(b) El Salvador : The Secretariat sent a draft agreement together with background notes on 
privileges and immunities to the NDA in September 2015. The Secretariat met with the 
El Salvador delegation during COP  21 in December 2015, after which the draft 
agreement with backgr ound note was resent to the NDA. In October 2018, the 
Secretariat met with NDA to provide information about the draft agreement and the 
rationale for privileges and immunities, and clarified queries regarding the importance 
for the GCF of signing such agre ements. However, no formal response has been received 
so far;  
(c) Kenya : The Secretariat sent a draft agreement on privileges and immunities, together 
with a background note, to the Treasury Department of Kenya on March 2016, and 
several exchanges have taken p lace to respond to comments received from the Attorney 
General’s Office and the Ministry of Foreign Affairs of Kenya. However, no formal response or written comments on the draft agreement have been received thus far;  
(d) Malawi : The Secretariat sent a draft agreement together with background notes on 
privileges and immunities to the Ministry of Foreign Affairs in September 2015 and met 
with the delegation of Malawi at COP  22 in December 2016 to provide information about 
the draft agreement and GCF status. However, no formal response has been received 
thus far;  
(e) North Macedonia : the Secretariat sent a draft agreement together with background 
notes on privileges and immunities to the Ministry of Finance and the Ministry of 
Environment and Physical Planning in Apri l 2017, and met with the Macedonian 
delegation at the GCF structured dialogue held in Tajikistan on September 2018. 
However, no formal response on the draft agreement has been received thus far;  
(f) Panama: The Secretariat sent a draft agreement together with background notes on 
privileges and immunities to the Minister of Presidency in September 2017. In October 
2018, the Secretariat met with the NDA and provided information about the draft 
agreement and its rationale. The Secretariat has sent a follow -up emai l in November 
2020; however, no formal response has been received so far ; 
(g) Sao Tome and Principe : The Secretariat sent a draft agreement together with 
background on privileges and immunities to the NDA in February 2017 and met with the 
Sao Tome and Principe ’s delegation at the Global Programming Conference in August

114. The Heads of the Independent Redress Mechanism and Independent Integrity Unit have 
both expressed that it would not be legally feasible to undertake their redress acti vities and/or 
investigations, as appropriate, in countries where the GCF is not provided with relevant 
privileges and immunities. Therefore, it is recommended that disbursements by the GCF are 
made only after the GCF has obtained satisfactory protection ag ainst litigation and 
expropriation in the country(ies), or has been provided with appropriate privileges and immunities.  
115. The definition of the term “Executing Entities” in the AMA includes entities that channel 
GCF proceeds and/or carry out project impleme ntation. Under the proposed programme, for 
each project the AE will enter into a subsidiary agreement with the relevant host country that 
will carry out project implementation and/or channel the GCF proceeds to a “project 
implementing entity” that will imp lement all or part of the project . Therefore, as per the terms 
of the AMA, each of the host countries and the project implementing entities should be considered as Executing Entities for the purposes of complying with the obligations to be 
imposed on Execu ting Entities under the AMA and the FAA. During the course of negotiations on 
the proposed programme it has become clear that the AE will not accept the project implementing entities to be considered as Executing Entities and, therefore, they will not sign  a 
subsidiary agreement with each of them. However, i n terms of contractual arrangements, the AE 
has agreed that it will enter into a subsidiary agreement (as per the terms of the AMA) with 
each host country , and that it will also enter into a direct “proj ect agreement” with each project 
implementing entity  that will set out the relevant obligations of an Executing Entity set out in 
the AMA and the FAA.

Independent Technical Advisory Panel’s assessment  of 
FP177 
Proposal name:  Cooling Facility  
Accredited entity:  World Bank  
Country/(ies):  Bangladesh, El Salvador, Kenya, Malawi, North Macedonia, 
Panama, Sao Tome and Principe, Somalia,  and Sri Lanka  
Project/programme size:  Large  
I. Assessment of the independent Technical Advisory Panel  
1.1 Impact potential                 Scale: Medium to low 
1. This is a large  size funding proposal submitted by the World Bank as the accredited 
entity (AE).  The programme covers nine countries from different regions: four  countries in 
Africa (Kenya, Malawi, Sao Tome and Principe, Somalia);  two countries in Asia ( Bangladesh , Sri 
Lanka);  one country from Eastern Europe (North Macedonia); and two countries from Latin 
America ( El Salvador, Panama).  The funding proposal is for the establishment of  a cooling  
programme or/and facility1 with the aim of reduc ing greenhouse gas ( GHG)  emissi ons from the 
cooling sector . Energy consumption by the cooling sector is increasing as a result of  
urbanization, increas ing income of population s and increasing  demand for cooling due to global 
warming -related local processes.  This is a multi -sector and mu lti-country programm e to 
establish a financing mechanism to address the challenge s of providing access to cooling while 
minimizing negative climate impact s. 
2. The programme aims to c ontribute to shifting from less -efficient cooling systems/no 
cooling system s (the baseline situation ) to more efficient cooling systems,  through a number of 
subprojects. The f unding proposal is submitted as a cross- cutting project:  77 per cent  of GCF 
proceeds is for GHG mitigation activities in the GCF result s area “buildin gs, cities, industries and 
appliances” and the remaining 33 per cent  is for GCF adaptation activities in the result s area 
“infrastructure and built environment ”. 
3. Increas ing energy demand has various drivers and among them is  increasing cooling 
requirement s driven by urgent  adaptation needs caused by increasing trends of cooling degree 
days ( CDD ), heat waves and days with  extremely high  maximum  temperatures . Of the  nine 
countries covered by the funding proposal only four are considered eligible for adaptation 
activities due to high vulnerability: Bangladesh, El Salvador, Malawi  and Somalia.  
4. GCF Financial support . The total cost of funding proposal is USD 879 .8 million 
including USD 157 million GCF contribution, which is 17.8  per cent  of total cost. Twenty per cent  
(USD 32 million)  of the total GCF contribution is grant  for technical assistance , credit lines and 
concessional loans.  The rest , 80 per cent  (USD 125 million) , is for a senor concessional loan . This 
loan has two different conditions:  USD 29 million (23 per cent ) has high concession ( 40 years  
tenor with  10 years of grace period  and no interest rate ) and the rest USD 96 million ( 77 per 
cent ) will be issue d for 20 years  with  5 years ’ grace perio d and  an interest rate of 0.75 per cent . 
 
1 According to the definition in the funding proposal, “The Cooling Facility is essentially designed as an umbrella 
facility (a Program) that enables aggregating, through a programmatic approach, different types and sizes of 
sustainable cooling interventions”.


---
*Page 133*
---

 
GCF/B. 30/02/Add. 09 
Page 133 
 
 
The total contribution from the AE is USD 722.84 million of which 15 per cent  is grant  
comprising contributions from the International Development Agency ( IDA), the Global 
Financing Facility , the Health Emergency Preparedness and R esponse Trust Fund  (HEPRTF)  and 
the Pandemic Emergency Fund,  and 7  per cent  is guarantee from the International Bank for 
Reconstruction and Development  (IBRD ) which is not still decided at the stage of submission  
and review .2 The remaining 78 per cent  is sen ior loans from IBRD and IDA with different 
pricing, grace periods and tenors  (for details see section C.1 of the funding proposal ). 
1.1.1.  Implementation arrangements  
5. The funding proposal states that , for the purposes of  the loans and grants  execution, 
executing entities (EE) will be selected in  the host countries , acting through the relevant 
ministry(ies) , or the host country may designate a  project implementing entity (PIE) to 
implement components 1  and 2, as identified and confirmed during p roject preparation. 
Accordingly , the funding proposal provides the list of EEs:  Bangladesh (Ministry of Finance, 
acting through the Ministry of Power, Energy and Mineral Resources), El Salvado r (Ministry of 
Finance, acting through the Ministry of Health), Kenya (National Treasury of the Republic of 
Kenya, acting through the Ministry of Energy and Petroleum), Malawi (Ministry of Finance, 
Economic Planning and Development, acting through the Mini stry of Industry, Trade and 
Tourism), North Macedonia (Ministry of Finance, acting through the Ministry of Economy), Panama (Ministry of Economy and Finance, acting through the Ministry of the Presidency) , 
Somalia (Ministry of Finance, acting through the M inistry of Health) and Sri Lanka (Ministry of 
Finance, acting through the Ministry of Power) .  
6. Three types of financial scheme  are considered for implement ing this programme : 
public procurement and contracting ; credit line ; and an energy efficiency financing facility . 
7. Public procurement and contracting : for cooling infrastructure service delivery and 
technical assistance. This mod el is relevant for components 1 and 3  of the funding proposal . 
This model will be practically implemented by th e AE and includ es contracting with providers 
of goods and service s, whereby contracts for services, works and goods could be submitted by 
an EE/PIE to the AE, which will assess them for relevancy and approve payments . Operation 
and maintenance contract s could be man aged by the EE/PIE or the EE could ensure that the 
final beneficiary ha s relevant capacity to maintain the equipment or infrastructure operation. 
GCF funding will be priced lower than and blended with W orld Bank funds, to reduce the overall 
financing cost , thereby absorbing the incremental cost of deploy ing efficient cooling solutions. 
Final beneficiaries , in this financial model, could be households, sole traders, micro, small and 
medium- sized  enterprises, or communities. Through these interventions, GCF concessional 
funds would play a catalytic role in creating a market for and increasing access to clean, efficient and sustainable cooling . 
8. Credit lines: these are considered for less mature markets . The Cooling  Facility will 
support the provision of credit lines for mitigation of the perceived high financial risk of energy -
efficient and clean cooling, which may be provided  through energy service companies (ESCOs). 
The AE will channel GCF finance along with its c o-financing to the EE ( i.e. the host country as the 
borrower/ recipient), through a subsidiary agreement. The EE would extend the credit line to 
one or more partner financial institutions (PFIs), which would typically be banks or micro -
lending institutions,  for on -lending to eligible sub -borrowers  such as  micro, small and medium -
sized enterprises (for example, industry and other private sector entities).  In doing so, the PFIs 
will select and approve sub -loan applications based on the agreed investment criteria . 
Subsidiary agreements would require the EE to ensure that the PFIs comply with the sublending 
and other terms, and that they monitor the performance of the PFIs.   
 
2 Together IDA and IBRD form the World Bank. IDA focuses on the world’s poorest countries, while IBRD assists 
middle -income and credit -worthy poorer countries.


---
*Page 134*
---

 
GCF/B. 30/02/Add. 09 
Page 134 
 
 
9. The independent Technical Advisory Panel (TAP) notes  that the implementa tion 
efficiency of this model depends significantly on the capacity of PFIs to manage climate change 
products  locally . The AE has  not yet conducted baseline studie s for each participant country , 
including the assessment of PFI market capacity, so the indep endent TAP  considers that this 
model has high risk. Moreover, barriers to improving the efficiency of the cooling sector vary 
from countr y to country  and this has  not been  thoroughly  assessed and presented in the funding 
proposal. The independent TAP  notes that an u nderstanding of the country -specific barriers to 
energy efficiency  products should be the basis for develop ing the standard investment criteria 
for lending. The i nformation provided in the funding proposal is general and does  not enable the 
independent TAP  to assess whether this model of credit line will be successful , because of the 
lack of well -developed investment criteria for country -specific cooling product s. Capacity -
building programmes for PFIs  and standard tools for th e assessment of product eligibility for 
lending could significantly reduce the risks related to the  capacities  of PFIs . Unfortunately , the 
funding proposal is not transparent on this subject. The independent TAP  notes the following 
transparency issues :  
(a) The AE  should transparently clarif y whether or not the PFIs are only local financial 
institution s. Such a clarification would provide information to assess the potential 
capacity of the PFIs;  
(b) The funding proposal states that the PFIs are assessed by the EEs using eligibility 
criteria and eight such criteria are provided in funding proposal (p.37) using Malawi  as 
an example of how the criteria are applied . However, the independent TAP  noted that 
none of these eight criteri a assess the capacity  and/or experience of a PFI to manage 
climate change related or energy -efficien t product s; and 
(c) A second set of criteria f or energy efficiency  product investment is mentioned in the 
funding proposal but transparent details are not provi ded.   
10. Energy efficiency financing facility : Under this model, the GCF proceeds are 
channelled  through the EE (the host country as the borrower) according to the terms of the 
subsidiary agreement signed with W orld Bank. The EE either directly, or through a PIE, on -
lends the funds to sub -borrowers ( e.g. private energy service companies, municipalities and 
small and medium -sized enterprises) to undertake eligible cooling investments. The PIE would 
extend subloans, partial guarantees or grants, as applicable, to sub -borrowers (e.g. energy 
service companies or cooling -as-a-service companies).  
11. The energy e fficiency financing facility  could have two implementation modalities:  
(a) Public super ESCO model : In this model , the h ost country designates a public energy 
service company (the public super ESCO) as the PIE. Th is super ESCO would typically be 
a public entity established by the government to ( 1) support cooling and energy 
efficiency investments in the public sector (public buil dings and sustainable cooling 
projects) ; and ( 2) strengthen private sector ESCOs through demand aggregation, bulk 
procurement of equipment (therefore reducing the cost of equipment for ESCOs), 
standardization of energy -saving performance contract s, monitor ing and verification  
templates and capacity -building, project development, and facilitation ; and  
(b) Energy efficiency revolving facilities : This modality combines the features of a plain  
(vanilla ) financing facility and the super ESCO model . In addition to providing financing 
to eligible sub -borrowers, the PIE would invest in energy efficiency activities using its 
own capital ( e.g. for retrofitting of existing buildings) . 
1.1.2.  Analysis of components  
12. The components described in the funding proposal  are structured to help countries 
remove the barriers to sustainable cooling by helping to address the financing barrier  and build


---
*Page 135*
---

 
GCF/B. 30/02/Add. 09 
Page 135 
 
 
an enabling environment through such activities as informing policy and regulato ry 
frameworks, reaching out to stakeholders through effective communication s, and supporting 
training and capacity -building. As such, the independent TAP  is of the opinion that this funding 
proposal is not a typical “programme” but is more of a facility, as reflected in the name of the 
proposal. As described in the funding proposal, the Cooling Facility will  consist of three main 
components , which will be calibrated according to countries’ needs and in a ccordance with the 
Facility’s criteria . Component 1 is mainly capacity -building, component 2 is the investment 
component and component 3 is the management component ( fully financed by the AE ). 
13. Component 1: Support to policy, regulatory and enabling environment . The total cost of 
this component is USD 91.6 million, of which USD 15.7 million  (17 per cent ) is GCF grant , USD 
46.1  million  (50 per cent ) is the senor loan  and USD 21.6  million (23.6 per cent ) is grant from 
the AE . The remaining USD 8.25 million ( 9.4 per cent ) will be  grant from the HEPRTF  and the 
Global Financing Facility.   
14. Activities supported under component 1 may include, for instance, ( 1) capacity -building 
of key stakeholders ( e.g. on technologies; business models; and cooling project  appraisal, 
implementation and evaluation); ( 2) technical assistance for developing plans, strategies, 
policies, regulations and standards; ( 3) designing sustainable market mechanisms and business 
models; and ( 4) communications and awareness campaigns.  
15. Com ponent 2 : Financing for cooling investments . The total cost of this component is 
USD  769.04 million , comprising  an USD 84.24 million ( 11 per cent ) grant  from GCF and the AE , 
and USD 50 million ( 6.5 per cent ) guarantee from the AE,  with USD 634.8 million ( 82.5 per cent ) 
as a sen ior loan ( 19.6 per cent  from GCF and  80.4 per cent  from the AE) . This component is the 
facility/financial scheme for financing of climate change eligible mitigation and adaptation 
projects. The deployment of these funds will  be tailored to the needs of the projects, through 
suitable financing modalities for each project’s context ; for example, credit lines to financial 
intermediaries, or loans to households, municipalities and small, medium and large enterprises, 
or subsidies. The s econd and third financial models are eligible for this component. Component 
2 will finance the projects that comply with  the following condition and project eligibility 
criteria:  
(a) Condition . The funding proposal states that the Cooling Facility will allocate a maximum 
amount equivalent to an aggregate of 30  per cent  of the total GCF proceeds to any given 
host country /project3, regardless of the financial instrument(s) deployed . The 
independent TAP  consider s that this condition could be a threat for some countries 
among these nine  wishing to participate but with  relatively  low capacities, if four or five  
leader developing countries take up over half of this  cap. The funding proposal does  not 
clarif y how this 30  per cent  maximum threshold per country was  calculated. The d raft 
term sheet demonstrates that th is criterion  balanced by the S ecretariat having 
establish ed an indicative amount for each GCF eligible country which has already 
provided a no -objection letter. Th e indicative amounts of the GCF proceeds are also 
secured by the term sheet, which establish es the cap for eligible  reallocation of 
indicative amount s (15 per cent ). According to the term sheet , a new country cannot be 
added to these nine countries  and – crucially – the eligibility criteria  (items (b) –(i) 
below)  are no longer  relevant for selection of a new country/ project (as it is defined by

the AE) . However, these criteria c ould be applied by the AE to replace  sub-projects 
within these nine countries ; 
(b) Cooling investments . Projects selected under the Cooling Facility will support 
investments in the following target areas: ( 1) space cooling and cool/green solutions; 
and ( 2) refrigeration, cold chain  storage  and logistics. In the case of ( 1), projects should 
demonstrate a pathway towards financially sustainable models that reduce the need for 
public resources over time and eventually leverage commercial and private sector 
funding . In the case of investing in off -grid cold storage capacity additions (under ( 2) 
refrigeration, cold storage), the EE will need to demonstrate that the main source of 
energy to power these additional cold storage facilities is renewable energy (e.g. solar);  
(c) World Bank operation linkag e. A Cooling Facility -supported project may (1 ) co-
finance a new IDA or IBRD operation; or ( 2) be additional financing to an existing IDA or 
IBRD operation. The ratio of GCF funds to IDA/IBRD funds for each supported project 
will be at least 1:1 . The indep endent TAP  noted that i t should be made very clear how 
the GCF proceeds that are spen t for cooling purposes will be monitored when proceeds 
are added to the existing funds , even if those funds are for energy efficiency . Energy 
efficiency measures for heati ng purposes not always efficient for cooling. In some cases, 
it might be vice -versa and heating energy efficiency measures facilitate the increase of 
cooling energy consumption (due to location of building in favour of heating EE or other heating EE measur es). For example, i n the case of North Macedonia , the GCF proceeds 
are planned to be  added to the existing energy efficiency  fund. The independent TAP  
notes that c ooling efficiency monitoring indicators should be transparently described  
and separated from other monitoring indicators ; 
(d) Perform ance threshold . The threshold is stated as “a t least 20  per cent  energy 
efficiency improvement and/or at least 20  per cent  avoided/reduced GHG  emissions” . 
According t o a clarification provided by the AE this 20  per cent  will be results for the 
total project. A project , according to the definition confirmed  during the review , is in 
most cases a group of different activities . The funding proposal clarifies that for each 
project an estimate of GHG reduction impact should be developed based on publicly 
available GHG methodologies and calculation tools , and consistent with guidelines 
outlined in a nnex 24  of the funding proposal . Regarding this criteri on, the independent 
TAP notes  that 20 per cent  energy savings in some low -emission countries will not have 
any tangible impact on emission reduction and therefore the word “or” in the statement 
of performance threshold is not relevant : the 20 per cent  GHG reduction should be 
obligatory and the 20 per cent  energy savings should be regarded as desirable . As 
mentioned above , the monitoring and verification  of the results of this programme will 
be complicate d. Country baseline s and national statistic s related to the cooling process 
should be developed for each participant country;  
(e) Alignment with the transition to low g lobal warming potential  (GWP ) 
refrigerants :(Note that this is not a general criterion and should be applied only  to the 
subprojects considering refrigerators in the cooling process -TAP)  Projects  supported by 
the Cooling Facility will be aligned with the goals and requirements of the Kigali 
Amendment to the Montreal Protocol, under which hydrofluorocarbon ( HFC ) 
refri gerant s are being phased down and replaced with refrigerants that do less damage 
to the climate . Investments will support  the following activities: (1) providing cooling 
services without the use of refrigerants (e. g. fans and passive cooling solutions); and/or 
(2) using refrigerants that are aligned with the Kigali A mendment and meet relevant 
safety standards, as commercially available in respective local markets ;4

(f) Adaptation to climate change . (Note that t his criterion should be applied onl y for 
adaptation projects  -TAP.) The host country will be considered vulnerable to climate 
change  if facing extreme heat risk hazards, and  in severe need of cooling services. The 
Cooling Facility  will support projects that will bring cooling -related benefi ts associated 
with services provided by the buildings, cold chain  storage and logistic s, health facilities 
and other related cooling investments . As mentioned above , four countries (Bangladesh, 
El Salvador, Malaw i and Somalia ) are considered countries eligible for adaptation 
countries . The independent TAP  questioned the AE on the performance threshold for 
adaptation cases. The response from the AE clarified that there is no threshold  to entry  
for adaptation projects as far as f ocus of the programme is mitigation activities. The 
independent TAP  is of the opinion that if a  funding proposal claims adaptation impact as 
well , this adaptation effect should be more transparent and a threshold should be 
established  such as  the number of  direct beneficiaries that will have improved  access to 
cooling. The independent TAP  further noted that, for example,  El Salvador is not listed 
among most vulnerable countries in the latest report about high -risk countries5 and it 
should be clarified why this country considered under adaptation eligible countries.  
(g) Paradigm shift potential . Supported projects will propose activities/technolog ies/ 
applications that aim to achieve market transformation, create new markets and 
business activities at the local, national or international level, and can potentially be 
replicated in other sectors or geographic areas.  The independent TAP  is of the opinio n 
that this criteri on is formulated  appropriately , though some of the ex ample 
interventions/  measures provided in annex 22 of the funding proposal do  not have such 
potential , but among them are activities related to cold chains  storage and logistics , 
refri gerators and COVID -19 vaccination processes  which could have transformative 
effect  if successful ly implement ed;  
(h) Country ownership and alignment with country priorities . Facility -supported 
projects will be aligned with country priorities and climate change /cooling action plans 
as presented in national development plans or other policy documents . This criteri on is 
in line with GCF criteria ; however , cooling needs as such are not mentioned separately 
in countries’  strategies and action plans related to climate change or energy efficiency. 
Of the nine countries, o nly Bangladesh  and Panama  target efficient cooling measures in 
their building energy efficiency programme s; and 
(i) GCF concessionality level . The funding prop osal states that concessionality levels 
would be assessed based on the pricing of the W orld Bank co-financing, risk of external 
debt distress of the  host country as published by the I nternational Monetary Fund  under 
the joint W orld Bank-IMF debt sustainabi lity framework, and the financial analysis of 
the projects  using  the following principles:  
(i) Grant only . Countries in external debt distress and for which the W orld Bank co-
financing is exclusively grant -based, would receive 100  per cent  grant from the 
Facility;  
(ii) Combination of high concessional loans and grants . Projects would meet any of 
the following three criteria: (1) host country in high risk of debt distress; (2) 
non-revenue generating project or where the reflows from the ultimate 
beneficiaries are not cost -reflective ; and (3) projects that target vulnerable 
communities or groups that are more vulnerable than the overall population 
(e.g. refugees, people in rural ar eas with low economic income);  
(iii) Financing terms:  40-year maturity, 10 -year grace period with an all -in fee of 0.25 
per cent  (i.e. 0.00 per cent  interest rate, 0 .00 per cent  commitment fee, 0.25 per

16. The independent TAP  is of the opinion that not all of these eligibility criteria are suitable 
for project/subproject selection because they are a mix of investment criteria, conditions, and 
so on . The independent TAP  considers that the se criteria should be regrouped and titled and 
revised according to the term sheet terminology.  
17. Component 3: Project management. The total cost ( 100 per cent ) of this component is 
covered by the AE and it equates to USD 19.2 million or 2 per cent  of the total project cost. Some 
39 per cent  of this cost is the W orld Bank senior loan and the remainder ( 61 per cent ) is grant 
from the AE and HEPR TF. 
18. This component will support the EEs and PIEs in the management, coordination and 
implementation of project activ ities, in compliance with their  contractual obligations with the 
AE. 
1.1.3.  Expected impact  
19. The independent TAP  notes that the project management unit, described in f igure 4 of 
the funding proposal,  is not appropriate for achiev ing the climate change related results  
anticipated . Instead , the climate change team  responsible for implement ing the subprojects 
selection criteria and design ing the monitoring process should be added to the AE’s 
management unit  and similar representation  should be required fro m each EE.  
20. The programme could significantly contribute to the health -care sector of most 
vulnerable countries facing the lack of capacit y to deal with  COVID -19 vaccination  
requirements.   
21. Annual GHG emission reduction s estimated by the funding proposal am ount to 912,659 
tonnes of carbon dioxide equivalent ( tCO 2eq) per year , or 16,240,202 tCO 2eq for the 27-year 
lifetime of the actions funded under the Cooling F acility (15  year s lifespan for appliances and 
cold chains,  storage and logistic and 20 years for buildings). The funding proposal calculates 
that there would be 4.22 million d irect beneficiaries and 16.85 million indirect beneficiaries. 
However, these estimated beneficiaries relate only to the projects in four vu lnerable  countries  
(Bangladesh, El Salvador, Malawi and Somalia)  at high risk of extreme heat hazards.  The 
independent TAP  notes that this approach is conservative , considering  that other countries also 
have beneficiaries that will receiv e improved cooling  appliances and cold chain  benefits . 
22. The independent TAP  scores this criterion as medium  to low , taking account of the fact  
that the impact of the funding proposal as a programme is not expected to be high. For example, 
some individual countries asking for  support for the health sector could  benefit from  a relatively 
high impact but for the other countries, without relevant baseline information the expectation is 
that impact will be low.  
1.2 Paradigm shift potential     Scale: High  
23. According to the feasibility study  provided by the AE,  global energy use for space cooling 
is projected to grow three -fold  between 2016 and 2050 , with a majority of this growth 
occurring in developing countries. While the growing need for space cooling is in alignment 
with  the developmental needs of countries, this growth must be addressed with effectively  
designed strategies and solutions to avoid severe economic, power system and environmental 
impacts.


---
*Page 139*
---

 
GCF/B. 30/02/Add. 09 
Page 139 
 
 
24. The Kigali Amendment to the Montreal Protocol entered  into  force  in 1 January  2019. 
The phase  down  of HFC refrigerants  under  the amendment  has the potential  to avoid  up to 0.1˚C  
of warming  by 2050  and up to 0.4˚C by 2100 .6 
25. The independent TAP  recognize s that an increase in cooling demand could be regarded 
as a climate change adaptation process that is particularly important for th ose countries most 
vulnerable  to the increasing trends of heat indexes  (maximum daily temperature, CCD s and heat 
waves). Mitig ation of this increas ing rate of cooling demand  through implementation of energy 
efficiency measures or by replac ing conventional fuel sources with renewable sources in th e 
cooling supply process will contribut e to the low -emission sustainable development pathway , 
with the potential of global  impact.  The independent TAP  shares the AE’s rationale that enabling 
those countries most vulnerable to climate change to meet the increasing cooling requirement 
should be considered as adaptation. Hence, the independent TAP  confirms that the concept 
considered within the funding proposal has  paradigm sh ift potential  in each country and 
globally . 
26. The Cooling Facility considered in the funding proposal plans  to demonstrate different 
options and in -country potentials for paradigm shift by proposing  investments across th e 
different but key cooling target areas and with a range of countries from different continents  of 
the developing world having different barriers and national priorities. Diversity of financial 
mechanism s for implement ing measures is another testing point in operation. I n the opinion of 
the AE such a diversified approach could amplify the potential of the AE to leverage its 
experiences and learning. The AE’s understanding is that in so doing, to provide pioneering 
example models and approaches, results can be scaled up and be fit -for-purpose in more 
countries with similar circumstances.  
27. The independent TAP  does not fully agree with the AE on its position that the selected 
approach of putting under one programme a  variety of  countries with different national 
circumstances and priorities and a variety of GHGs mitigation measures , as well as a number of 
complicate d financial schemes for implementation will be a more efficient way to put this 
subsector on a transformation al/sust ainable pathway compared with the traditional 
programmatic approach whe re countries and measures are concentrated  around the narrower 
concept than  cooling demand in general, in different sectors and with different sources.  Thus , 
the independent TAP  conclu des that the funding proposal is the pilot case for testing the 
effective approaches for mitigation of GHG emission s from th e cooling sector in a way that does 
not limit but fully satisf ies the climate change adaptation demand s of vulnerable population s 
and countries.  
28. Based on the above analysis the independent TAP  concludes that this funding proposal 
as a pilot programme could  not be rated as hav ing a high paradigm shift impact ; rather, more 
demonstration impact  is expected , providing examples of succe ss and fail ure and reasons  for 
these outcomes . The concept itself has high transformational potential.  
1.3 Sustainable development potential      Scale:  Medium  
29. Space cooling is increasingly recognized as a development priority and an issue of equity 
in a warm ing world. The i ncreasing trend of cooling demand and the increasing gap between 
energy supply and cooling demand is significantly exacerbated by COVID -19 management 
requirements.  
30. As mentioned in the feasibility study  (annex 2a),  meet ing cooling demand in a business-
as-usual way leads to increas ing GHG emission s, but providing cooling  is unavoidable if 
countries are to sustain resilience to climate change trends. The funding proposal describes how 
 
6 See the Climate & Clean Air Coalition website at https://www.ccacoalition.org/en/initiatives/efficient -cooling .


---
*Page 140*
---

 
GCF/B. 30/02/Add. 09 
Page 140 
 
 
the Cooling Facility contributes to susta inable development by demonstrating the options for 
slowing the increasing trend of energy consumption through supporting energy efficiency and 
clean energy development processes in pilot countries , transfer of clean technologies and 
solutions, and strengt hening capacity  in and awareness of cooling technologies.  At the country 
level this programme is particularly important for countries that are highly vulnerable to 
increasing temperature and heat stress as well as for countries with energy supply challenge s. 
From the global perspective the concept considered in the funding proposal on sustainable 
cooling supply deserves the urgent attention from global financial institutions.  
31. Supply of sustainable cooling d irectly contribute s to four  of the Sustainable 
Development Goals (SDGs):  
(a) SDG 3 – Good health and well -being . From the sample pro jects  attached to the fun ding 
proposal  three are for mitigation of health sector stress due to the necessary COVID -19 
management processes ; 
(b) SDG 7 – Affordable and clean energy . Off -grid solar panels provide clean and affordable 
energy to households and small farmers mainly in rural areas , ensuring the cold chain 
for food security purposes;  
(c) SDG 11 – Sustainable cities and communities. The programme’s contribution to this g oal 
is significant , particularly for countries focusing on energy efficiency in the building s 
sector;  and 
(d) SDG 13  – Climate actions. Increase in cooling demand  is exacerbated by climate change , 
and should be manag ed in a sustainable way in order to avoid further contribut ing to 
climate change process es. According to the information provided in the funding 
proposal if cooling efficiency remains unchanged, additional power generation capacity 
of 2,500 GW will be needed to meet electricity demand for air conditioning by 2050 – an 
estimated cost of US D 1.7 trillion. Increased access to cold chains is critical to ensuring 
food safety and reducing food loss and waste. By maximizing energy efficiency in the 
cooling sector (refrige ration and space cooling) while simultaneously phasing down 
HFCs contained in refrigerants, the world can potentially avoid close to 1 °C temperature 
increase by 2100.  
32. The programme offers different financial schemes  to counties and clean technology 
develo pers  for implement ing/disseminat ing highly  efficient cooling technologies ; thereby  
strengthen ing the capacities of local financial institutions for manag ing funding for cooling.  
33. Refrigerants in vapour compression -based equipment used in refrigeration and air 
conditioning account for a bout 20 per cent  of the climate impact associated with cooling, owing 
to direct emissions of HFC s.7 The Kigali Amendment targets global consumption of HFCs ,8 and 
could avoid up to 0.5°C of warming by 2100. The independent TAP  notes that this  is the first 
funding proposal  in the GCF process  targeting new industrial gases.  
34. The independent TAP  considers that the role of this subsector in the sustainable 
development p rocess is crucial and should have high  priority , although the independent TAP  is 
not sure that this particular programme , as it is designed , could have high impact at the country 
level and therefore scores this criterion as medium  to low .  
 
 
7 G20 agreement to reduce greenhouse gases vital to tackle climate change – UN agency | | UN News  
8 The Kigali Amendment to the Montreal Protocol requires countries to phase down HFCs, beginning in 2019 for 
developed countries, and for most developing countries five years later. Its initial phase -down schedule will achieve 
a reduction in HFC consumption of more than 80 per cent by 2047. Most developed countries begin in 2019, 
whereas mos t developing countries will freeze consumption and production in 2024 and begin the phase  down five 
years later. The aim is for developing countries’ susceptible to high ambient temperatures will freeze at 2028 and 
begin to phase down in 2032.

1.4 Needs of the recipient  Scale:  High  
35. The rise in temperature and lack of access to cooling will impact the health, education ,
p
roductivity  and economic development of nations. In 2019, the Intergovernmental Panel on
Climate Change reported that at 1.5 °C of warming, 2.3 billion people could be both exposed and
vulnerable to heat wave events .
36. The c ooling n eeds of the nine countries  involved in the programme  significantly differ
from each other , as described  below .
37. Bangladesh, El Salvador, Malawi and Somalia are particularly vulnerable to heat
hazards , increasing their adaptation needs  as follows .
38. Bangladesh  is highly exposed to extreme heat, dr ought and wildfires. Extreme heat
hazard is classified as high throughout the country, which indicates that prolonged exposure to
extreme heat, resulting in heat stress, is expected to occur at least once in the next five years .9
T
he seasonal frequency of  days with T max ≥ 36°C has been on the increase since 1986 in most
parts of Bangladesh . Average temperatures have been increasing more rapidly during th e
m
onsoon season , from June to August, according to historical data for the p ast 30 years (1991 –
2020). Forecast s show that  extreme temperatures are expected to occur with high frequency,
duration, or severity. Most notably, the total number  of hot days in a year (where dai ly
m
aximum temperature is above 35°С) is projected to increase by 13 days by 2039, by 22 days
by 2040 -59, and by 41 days by 2099. By mid -century, annual CDD s are projected to rise from
482 CDD (2020–2039) to about 778 CDD by 2059, and 1210 by the end of the century. Overa ll,
t
his will impact the energy sector because any time the temperature rises above 18.3°C, there is
an expected increase in power demand for cooling .10
39. Bangladesh is among the w orld’s most vulnerable countries to the effects of climate
change. The country is also facing serious energy security challenges and severe heat stress ,
w
ith the impact reaching its high -income residents, low -income neighbourhoods and informal
slum settlements. In its GCF country programme, for the mitigation secto r Bangladesh mainl y
f
ocus es on capacity -building needs for develop ing bankable projects , which need relevant dat a
a
nd tools for assess ing project parameters. Developing strong public –private partnership s,
leveraging private sector resources , and access to competitive and inclusive value chain
financial products and services are priority areas highlighted by the country.
40. The Climate Facility will support investments in sustainable cooling solutions for the
building sector as essential adaptation measures delivered through low- carbon efficient
solutions .
41. El Salvador is highly exposed to extreme heat, hurricanes, droughts and wildfires.
Extreme heat hazard is classified as high throughout the country, which indicates thatprolonged exposure to extreme heat, resulting in heat stress, is expected to occur at least onc
e
i
n the next five years .11 El Salvador has experienced a sustained increase in monthly
temperature s over the p ast 30 years (1991 –2020). In particular, the summer months  (i.e. April
to August) have  witnessed consistent increase s in temperature. According to the future  trends ,
mo
st notably, the number of days where the daily maximum temperature exceeds 35°C wi ll
i
ncrease by 36 days by the end of the 21st century, and the probability of heat waves will
increase by 30  per cent. The annual CDD s are projected to rise by 4 7 CDD by 2050, and 1 ,370.68
CDD by the end of the century .12 El Salvador is situated in the dry corridor of Central America,

44. Malawi  is prone to adverse climate hazards including extreme temperatures, dry spells ,
s

and 2006, mean annual temperature increased by 0.9°C . Climate trends over this period show
that the total number of hot days in a year has increased by 30 days. By mid -century, annual
CDDs  are projected to rise by 953, with the number of hot days in a year expected to increase by

45. In August 2020, “Malawi Financial Inclusion and Entrepreneurship Scaling” project  for
agriculture sector , was approved by the World Bank Board.  Refrigeration, cold storage and
logistics for agriculture and food security are planned  to be supported by the Cooling F acility
within the framework of this project . Additional funds from the Cooling Facility would help
strengthen climate change policy framework of the Government of Malawi to promote the use of
low- carbon cooling technologies  in other interventions  as well as  support cold chains for rural
farmers .
46. Somalia  is generally arid and semi -arid with two rainfall seasons. The country’s climat e
i
s influenced by the Inter -Tropical Convergence Zone. Between 1960 and 2020  mean annual
temp erature s have increased by about 0.5°C in the hottest months (April and May) and 0.3°C in
the coldest months (January and December). M uch of Somalia is at risk of several natur al
h
azards, including extreme heat and droughts. Risk of e xtreme heat hazard is considered high ,
w
ith prolonged exposure to extreme heat resulting in heat stress expected to occur at least onc e
i
n the next five years .15 The number of days where the daily maximum temperature exceeds
35°C is expected to increase by about 70 days by 2059  compared with  1986 –2005, and by 93
days by the end of the 21st century. Annual CDD s are expected to rise by more than 836
between 2040 and 2059, and by 1182 CDD s by the end of the century. This significant increas e
i
n temperatures will impact the energy s ector because there will be higher power demand for
cooling .
47. The Cooling Facility will support financing and implementing solar power systems for
space cooling and cold chains in primary - and secondary -level health -care facilities to addre ss

direct -drive refrigerators that can stand up to Somalia’s harsh climatic conditions to expand 
climate -friendly cold storage capacity for vaccines.  
48. Kenya  has included e nhancement of energy and resource efficiency as one of the 
priorit ies among mitigation options  in its nationally determined contribution ( NDC ). Despite the 
priority given to adaptation in Kenya’s NDC, the country recognizes the importance of clean, 
efficient and sustainable energy technologies to reduce over -reliance on fossil and non -
sustainable biomass fuel s. Kenya intends to include non- CO 2 gases such as HFCs and 
perfluorocarbons in its updated NDC.  
49. Off-grid  solar  access  for underserved  countie s is targeted by the Cooling F acility.  Such  
support  would  be used  for innovative  business  models  to make  cold  chains available  to small  
farmers  and to provide  financing  to mitigate  the high  up-front  cost  of efficient  air conditioning 
and refrigeration  systems  (solar powered  in off-grid  contexts)  for residential  and business  
applications.  In doing  so, it will promote  sustainable  refrigeration  and protect  against  extreme  
heat  in underserved  areas  of Kenya.  
50. In North Macedonia  most of  public buildings (including schools, health -care 
cent res/clinics and universities) are over 50 years old and classified as under -heated, unsafe 
(due to structural instability and seismic risks) and dilapidated. In fact, buildings, which 
consume about 39 p er cent  of the energy, have been identified in the country as a major priority, 
with estimates of savings ranging from 20  to 40 per cent . The public sector has the greatest 
potential, with about 35 –40 per cent  savings, mostly in the health and education se ctors . 
Several challenges need to be addressed for the public sector to invest in sustainable cooling. Central government buildings typically have very limited budgetary provisions for capital 
improvements, if any, and usually are not allowed to borrow. Municipalities must undergo the 
fiscal decentralization process and, even then, have stringent debt limitations which many have 
already reached. Few have formal credit ratings. In the context of the COVID- 19 situation, 
rehabilitation of health -care  facilities, with adequate air -conditioning systems, is  expected to 
become a higher priorit y.  
51. North Macedonia is seeking to tackle space cooling in the public sector – and lower its 
energy consumption and associated GHG emissions – by establish ing an energy efficiency fund, 
a sustainable financing mechanism that is well suited for its circumstances and targeted sector.   
52. Panama ’s cooling appliances market has been growing rapidly over the past decade, for 
both air condition ing and refrigerat ion technologies. Air conditioning penetration in the 
residential sector is relatively low but is expected to rapidly increase in line with the country’s dynamic economic development (e.g. residential household ownership of air -conditioning 
equipment stood at 8.9 per cent  in 2000, compared with 15.6 per cent  in 2010 and is expected 
to grow to an estimated 66 per c ent in 2050). According to the National Energy Plan of Panama 
(Plan Energético Nacional 2015 –2050), energy consumption for cooling systems currently 
represent s about 40 per cent  of the overall electricity consumption in Panama. The electricity 
sector is facing serious challenges in keeping up with rising electricity demand, which is growing at an average annual rate of 8.5 per cent  per year mainly due to the increased use of 
cool ing equipment in the residential and commercial sectors. In order to align with Panama’s 
NDC, the pace of energy demand growth needs to be reduced from an average of 6 per cent  in 
the business -as-usual scenario to around 4 per cent , which would reduce the need of energy 
generation capacity additions by 38 per cent . Several barriers must be addressed to respond to 
this rapidly growing problem. Although Panama has a sophisticated banking sector, the absence 
of (1) dedicated credit lines priced to incentivize investments in energy efficiency, and (2) a 
local long -term capital market for mortgages (access of local banks to capital markets is limited 
to up to 5 -year bonds), as well as (3) limited technical capacity in local banks to assess energy 
efficiency produ cts and investments, represent a major barrier for energy efficiency in Panama. 
The higher initial cost of energy -efficient equipment and lack of access to affordable financing


---
*Page 144*
---

 
GCF/B. 30/02/Add. 09 
Page 144 
 
 
are hindering the adoption of more sustainable cooling solutions and could lock -in large 
quantities of inefficient, polluting but cheaper (on a first -cost basis) cooling equipment. Law 69 -
12 mandates the establishment of an energy efficiency fund. The national Fondo de Eficiencia 
Energética (UREE Fund) aims to be transformational by cataly sing private investment, reducing 
the high  risk perception associated with green buildings and energy- efficient cooling appliances, 
and improv ing market liquidity for energy efficiency initiatives. Credit line facilities will help 
transform the resid ential and commercial sectors and leverage private sector capital.  
53. The Cooling Facility will channel GCF funds through the Government of Panama to 
provide credit lines to the Panama Energy Efficiency Fund and support the enabling 
environment.  
54. Sao Tome and  Principe  is a small low - to middle -income country compris ing two main 
islands. It has a total population of approximately 200,000 people, 42.6  per cent  of whom are 14 
years of age or younger. In addition to having a small population and a remote location, there is 
a high fixed cost of public goods  – all factors that affect the country’s trade, fiscal accounts and 
human development outcomes. The p ublic health sector is underdeveloped and this is a 
significant barrier to deal ing with COVID -19. International collaboration and support to the 
Government of Sao Tome and Principe will be crucial to continue mitigation measures to 
contain the spread of the pandemic in the country and to design and implement its National 
COVID -19 Immuni zation Plan . 
55. The Cooling Facility will support the health sector services through financing and 
implementing climate -friendly space cooling and cold chains in the country’s health -care 
network, with the overall objective that every health facility receives  a solar power system. 
Investments will include, for example , solar photovoltaics and battery systems to offset existing 
backup diesel generation, energy efficiency improvements in health facilities, walk -in cold 
rooms or cold -storage warehouses , and solar  direct -drive refrige rator s/freezers or other low -
GWP cold chain equipment . 
56. Sri Lanka . The introduction of d emand -side management activities is one of the priority 
areas  of Sri  Lanka’s NDC. Sri Lanka has recently taken strong initiatives to implement effic ient 
and effective sustainable energy programmes. Promotion of solar power generation has been 
initiated through different modalities such as solar rooftops and solar parks. This will provide 
approximately 500 MW in the short term and has the potential to expand to a considerably 
high er level by 2030. In addition to the interventions concerning power generation, the 
Government of Sri Lanka has also identified demand -side management as one of the priority 
areas, and a Presidential Task Force has been appointed for implementing an island -wide 
demand -side management programme encompassing all the related sectors.  
57. Improv ing energy efficiency in the building sector is the focus of  a sample project. The 
proposed project will finance and promote the impleme ntation of energy -efficient, climate-
friendly chillers and other energy efficiency measures in Sri Lanka’s commercial and industrial 
building sector.  
58. The independent TAP considers that the needs of the recipient countr ies are high but 
are not adequately es timated for most of the participant countries . Limited baseline details are 
provided for few countries. The i nformation provided above , extracted from the funding 
proposal and NDCs should be supported by and updated through  a baseline study. The 
independen t TAP scored this criteri on as high mainly based on the information provided for 
three highly vulnerable participant countries identified in the assessment report, “Tracking 
Sustainable Cooling for A ll”.16

1.5 Country ownership                Scale:  Medium  
59. All n ine countries involved in the programme have provided no -objection letters . In 
addition:  
(a) All of these countries have submitted their u pdated NDC;  
(b) All of these countries have energy efficiency as one of the priorit ies for implement ing 
their NDCs through their climate action plans;   
(c) Bangladesh and Panama are the only countries among these nine that have 
transparently claimed the cooling sector as priority for energy efficiency measures;  and  
(d) Only Bangladesh has finali zed and adopted a GCF country programme. Most of the other 
participant countries  are at different stages of application and preparation.  
60. Bangladesh : The Government has prepared the Energy Efficiency Conservation Master 
Plan 2015 –2030, which describes the ado ption and implementation of energy efficiency and 
conservation regulatory measures. Bangladesh is  developing a national Cooling Action Plan. 
These efforts are aligned with the Cooling Facility’s support of space cooling and energy 
efficiency measures in bu ildings.   
61. El Salvador : The Government is integrating climate -friendly investments in its national 
program mes, and its NDC includes health as a sector affected by climate change and the 
promotion of wide r use of renewable energy. This is aligned with the Cooling Facility’s support 
for climate -friendly health facilities and cold chain s. 
62. Kenya : Under the Paris Agreement, Kenya has committed to reducing GHG emissions by 
30 per cent , or 142 MtCO 2eq, by 2030. The count ry’s National Electrification Strategy presents a 
roadmap for achieving universal access to electricity by 2022. Supporting off -grid cooling 
appliances and access under the Cooling Facility will contribute to both of these goals. Kenya is 
also in the proce ss of developing a National Energy Efficiency and Conservation Strategy, which 
includes targets for energy savings in households, buildings and industry.  
63. Malawi : Promoting the use of energy -efficient technologies and designs is one of the 
strategies advocated in the Government’s priority areas under the Malawi Growth and 
Development Strategy.  
64. North Macedonia : As a contracting party of the Energy Community Treaty, the 
Government has committed to reduce energy use by 12  per cent  (about 200 kilo tonnes of oil 
equivalent ) by 2018 in its 2020 Energy Efficiency Strategy (based on a 2010 baseline), more 
than the 9  per cent  required under the Treaty’s National Energy Efficiency Action Plans. 
Buildings, which consume about 39  per cent  of energy  in the country, have b een identified as a 
major priority, with estimates of energy savings ranging from 20  to 40 per cent .  
65. Panama : In 2012, the Government made a national commitment to energy efficiency by 
approving Law 69 -12 on “Rational and Efficient Use of Energy”. In 2015, the National Secretariat 
of Energy published the National Energy Plan 2015 –2050, which contemplates the design and 
implementation of the Rational and Efficient Use of Energy Program me as one of the key actions 
to meet the sector objectives. The country al so ratified the Kigali Amendment in 2017. Panama 
is currently implementing stage II of its HFC Phase -out Management Plan under the Montreal 
Protocol. The Cooling Facility will support achieving the Government’s commitments through its 
support for efficient  space cooling.  
66. Sao Tome and Principe : The country’s NDC include s promoting the greater use of 
renewable energy and list s health as one of the sectors negatively impacted by climate change. 
The Cooling Facility will contribute to the Government’s NDC throu gh supporting space cooling 
and cold chains in the health sector, including through the increased use of solar power.


---
*Page 146*
---

 
GCF/B. 30/02/Add. 09 
Page 146 
 
 
67. Somalia : Somalia’s NDC on  scaling up the use of solar energy is consistent with the 
Cooling Facility’s support for more climate -friendly health facilities and cold chain s, including 
through greater use of solar power.  
68. Sri Lanka : Demand -side management is identified as one of the seven mitigation actions 
of the energy sector NDC of Sri Lanka,  to be implemented during 2020 –2030. Regarding the 
regulatory framework, the Energy Efficiency Building Code was first introduced in 2002 and 
revised in 2008. The code sets standards for energy efficiency in design and retrofits in 
buildings, while also  providing methods for determining compliance. 
69. According to the funding proposal, for the purposes of the loans and grants execution, 
energy efficiency will be selected in the host countries . This approach increase s countr ies’ 
ownership of the programme , although  the independent TAP  notes that the capacity of the EE s 
for implement ing cooling energy efficiency projects should be  regularly  assessed and capacity -
building support should be provided where gaps are identified.  
70. Based on the information provided in the count ries’ NDCs t he independent TAP 
considers that country ownership at this stage is medium. Almost all participant countries have 
energy efficiency as a priority , but they are not clearly focus ing on cooling needs at the strategy 
and policy levels. Exceptions among these ni ne countries are Bangladesh and Panama. A 
comprehensive baseline study might change this conclusion.  
1.6 Efficiency and effectiveness              Scale:  Medium to low 
71. The share of the GCF total contribution  to the total co -financing from the AE  and other 
donors is 1:4.6. For the loan , this proportion is 1:4.5 (USD 280 million is reported as  TBD  ) and 
for the grant , the  ratio is 1:6.3. Allocation of 100 per cent  of GCF loan and 51 per cent  of grant to 
component 2 is considered by the independent TAP  as eff ective  distribution of the investment  
by components . The GCF concessional loan fully supports the most risky investment for 
component 2.  
72. As already mentioned in section  1.1, the independent TAP ’s concern regarding the 
maximum 30 per cent  of GCF proceeds as a cap per country, is effectively  balanced  by the 
Secretariat in the term sheet and now all countries have some indicative budget. The 
independent TAP  welcome s this approach , as it considers t hat all GCF eligible countr ies issuing 
no-objection letters should have equal opportunity to participate in the prog ramme. Securing 
each country’s opportunity for equal participation makes the programme more effective from 
the countries ’ perspective.  
73. As me ntioned above , the projects and measures to be implemented through the Cooling 
Facility are too diverse and therefore it is not possible, or efficient , to design unique eligibility 
criteria and the associated climate change impact monitoring methodology. E nergy efficiency, 
renewable energy (solar) and GHG -free refrigerators are considered as eligible activities for this 
Cooling Facility ; these systems are  quite diverse , with each having  specificities that should be 
taken into consideration when design ing measures, financial schemes and monitoring 
modalities. Technical details are not provided for the  attached  example projects but  the main 
focus is on financial schemes.  These are important for GCF  but only if balanced with  
technical /technological elements and the funding proposal results . Therefore , it is difficult for 
the independent TAP to confirm that , even in the case of success of the proposed financial 
models , the mitigation and/or adaptation  results will be adequately  successful. Such diversity of 
the scope and technologies of the C ooling Facility as well as of the financial schemes  in one 
programme could have a negative impact on the programme’s overall efficiency and 
effectiveness as well as at the individual country  level. Grouping of nine  projects  (countries)  
considered in funding proposal is as follow:


---
*Page 147*
---

 
GCF/B. 30/02/Add. 09 
Page 147 
 
 
(a) Energy efficiency in the building sector , in some cases appliances are also included : 
Bangladesh, North Macedonia, Panama and Sri Lanka. Type s of buildings are not 
specified for  Bangladesh  but appliances are included ; only public buildings are the focus 
for North Macedonia ; 
(b) Efficient cooling facilities (HFC -free refrigerators, cold chains, logistic s): Kenya (off- grid 
solar energy  and energy access to  refrigeration, cold chains and logistics)  for cooling 
purposes, and Malawi  (refrigeration, cold chains and logistics in agriculture ); and  
(c) Strengthening the health sector within the context of COVID -19 requirements: El 
Salvador, Sao Tome and Principe, and Somalia.   
74. The funding proposal (on page 28) states that n ine countries are currently considering 
potential investments to be supported from the Cooling Facility. The World Bank will work with 
each EE covered under this Facility to finalize the scope and co -financing of these investment 
opportunities, taking into account ( 1) the needs of the clients as they may evolve;  (2) the results 
of the ongoing market assessments ; and ( 3) stakeholder consultations . As mentioned above , in 
the absence of proper market analysis and stakeholder consultations that includes the needs of 
the clients and without a final decision on the scope of activities,  the independent TAP is not 
disposed to assess and confirm the efficiency of programme to each participant countr y. 
75. Regarding the efficiency and effectiveness of the whole programme,  the independent 
TAP is of the  opinion that,  due to the broad diversity of the energy efficiency scope , the 
programme efficien cy is not expect ed to be high , in comparison  with some of the effect s in the 
individual countries asking for support in the health sector or cool chain for food security. Thr ee 
types of  findings could be considered in th e context  of the programme efficiency :  
(a) Cooling energy efficiency is a part of energy efficiency , but it should be treated 
separately and not mixed with traditional energy efficiency measures when building 
sector  efficiency  is being considered. This is a sector whe re efficiency measures for 
heating and cooling do not always coincide ; indeed, they  could contradict each other 
depending on the orientation of building s and type s of measures designed for heating 
energy efficiency . Therefore, to  automatically add  GCF  inve stment to an Energy 
Efficiency  fund ( as it is stated in the implementation modalities and demonstrated in the 
case of North Macedonia ) which is dedicated to the energy efficiency of the building  
without relevant revisions of the fund’s investment eligibility criteria for the cooling 
sector, the expec ted effect of the programme could be negative;   
(b) In countries where existing less -efficient appliances (including refrigerators ) will be 
replaced , the old appliances should be monitored and if they stay in second -hand use the 
increase in energy consumption related to the programme activities should be calculated and deducted from the reduced amount; and   
(c) Although the independent TAP  recognizes the urgency of such programmes for high ly 
vulnerable countries th at have limited access to cooling facilities under the pressing 
requirement of cooling due to COVID -19, this programme does  not demonstrate any 
added value compared with individual country -level projects and particularly for highly 
vulnerable countries wit h increasing trends of heat indices (CCD s, heat waves, extreme 
daily temperatures, etc.). The independent TAP  considers that in planning a 
programmatic approach the scope of targeted cooling technologies and geography and 
type of countries’ needs should be  narrowed on the bas is of baseline studies conducted 
for each country , to ensure the added value of adopting a programmatic approach.  
76. The independent TAP would particularly like to highlight the role of well -designed 
monitoring  process es in reaching high e fficiency and effectiveness from a programmatic 
approach comprising various financial schemes, components and subprojects. The m onitoring 
arrangements provided in a nnex 11 to the funding proposal are not satisfactory to the 
independent TAP . The independent TAP  would be interested to see more details of monitoring ,


---
*Page 148*
---

 
GCF/B. 30/02/Add. 09 
Page 148 
 
 
particularly for component 2 directly contributing to the final results. The AE should submit a 
very transparent monitoring plan for component 2 demonstrating how the final beneficiarie s 
(not always borrowers) – consumers of energy – will be monitored in general and in particular 
when subproject s consider many buildings, many appliances and so on . 
77. Based on the available information the independent TAP  considers that the efficiency 
and effectiveness of this programme is expected to be medium to  low. The independent TAP  
would also like to highlight that this programme is really a  “financial facility ” rather than a 
programme having added value to individual project . The independent TAP  noted  that it is 
receiving more and more facility and subfund type funding proposals based , in most cases , on 
good concept s and idea s but not supported by relevant feasibility/baseline studies. Such 
proposals are not fully fit to the programmatic approach  and not always relevant to assess 
against the six investment criteria  of the GCF . 
II. Overall remarks from the independent Technical Advisory Panel  
78. Despite the deficiencies identified by the independent TAP and commented on in the 
above assessment, the independent TAP  fully recognizes the urgent need to regulat e the cooling 
sector , which is not yet relevantly targeted as the most important subsector of the energy 
efficiency sector in terms of  successful implementation of the Paris Agreement and urgent 
mitigation -related  energy needs for COVID -19 vaccination -related cooling process es. The 
independent TAP  recomm ends this funding proposal for consideration and approval by the  GCF 
Board . Although recommending approval , the independent TAP also fully recognize s that the 
funding proposal has significant deficiences which should be addressed and improved prior to 
the execution of the funded activity agreement . The independent TAP recognizes that the 
ratings for the six crit eria are lower than usual for an endorsed funding proposal . However, the 
independent TAP  considers that, once the condition for endorsement is met, these ratings will 
go up. Hence, the independent TAP  recommends this funding proposal for approval under the  
following con dition:  
(a) Conditions to be met prior to the execution of the FAA:  
(i) Delivery by the AE to the GCF , in a form and substance satisfactory to the GCF 
Secretariat, of the following set of annexes, together with a relevantly revised 
funding proposal  pursuant to those annexes:  
(1) A cooling sector -related baseline study for each host country, which shall 
take into consideration (i) all the comments and recommendations 
expressed by the independent TAP  in this assessment report and (ii) the 
methodology for cooling sector baseline assessment considered  as the 
“guid ance ” in annex 02a ( Pimer for Space Cooling ) provided by the AE as 
part of the original submission;  
(2)  The baseline study for each host country  referred to in item (1) above 
shall estimate, take into account and set out: (i) ongoing  trends of 
cooling degree days and other heat indexes, for the last decades; (ii) 
correlation between the heat indexes and energy consumption trends for 
sectors where  activities are planned, taking into consideration impact of 
COVID -19 on energy demand; and (iii) trends of energy consumption by 
cooling sector and related trends of GHGs where statistics are available;   
(3) A comprehensive monitoring and verification plan f or demonstrating the 
mitigation of energy consumption and reduc tion of GHGs from the 
cooling sectors of the respective host countries compared with  the 
baseline situation. The monitoring methodology shall consider (i) 
monitoring of potential leakage and re lated  mitigation measures, and (ii)


---
*Page 149*
---

 
GCF/B. 30/02/Add. 09 
Page 149 
 
 
methodologies for monitoring the mitigation of power consumption at 
the final beneficiary level; and  
(4) Updated sample projects with description s of the baseline situation in 
the sub -sector specific to the relevant sample p roject and monitoring 
methodology specific to such sub -sector.

Response from the accredited entity to the independent 
Technical Advisory Panel's assessment (FP177)  
 
Proposal name:  Cooling Facility  
Accredited entity:  World Bank  
Country/(ies):  Bangladesh, El Salvador, Kenya, Malawi, North Macedonia, 
Panama, Sao Tome and Principe, Somalia, and Sri Lanka 
Project/programme size:  Large  
 
 
Impact  potential  
Scale: We consider the rating of “medium to low” (especially “low) as under -estimating the  
Facility’s  potential  impact.  The World  Bank  as AE is presenting  an innovative,  multi -sector  and 
multi -country  (covering  9 countries)  programmatic  financing  facility  to help  demonstrate  how  to 
address  the key sustainable  development  challenge  of providing  access  to cooling  in diverse  
contexts  while  minimizing  negative  climate  impacts.  We would  like to highlight  iTAP’s  
observation  that the approach  to estimating impact  in the FP is “conservative”.  
 
- Corrections/clarifications:  
o iTAP  notes  that “only four  countries  are considered  eligible  for adaptation  
activities ”. We would clarify that each of the 9 countries in the Facility are counted  
against  mitigation -related  indicators.  While  each  of the countries  covered by the 
Facility face the need to adapt to a warming world as they are exposed  to risks of 
extreme  heat  hazard, albeit  at different  intensity  levels, only the 4 countries  facing  
“high  risk”  of extreme  heat  hazard  (the  other  5 countries  face  “medium  risk”)  are 
counted  against  the Facility’s adaptation indicators.  
o In all projects, EE would be the Host country (“the Borrower”) acting through  the 
relevant ministry(ies) and/or designated Project Implementing Entities (PIEs).  
o The public  procurement  and contracting  model  may  be relevant  for components 1, 
2 and 3. This model will be implemented by the EE, or the PIE as may  be designated  
by the EE, (NOT  the AE) and as such,  already  corresponds  with  ITAP’s  
recommendation.  
o Rather than representing examples of projects, Annex 22 provides, for each  
country,  a summary  description  of the actual projects  that  are being  considered  
under the Cooling Facility, If the Facility is approved by the GCF Board and in  line 
with the notion of a program supporting projects are early development  stage, the 
AE, in consultation with each Host Country, will refine the scope the proposed  
activities considering  the agreed parameters.  
o The AE has provided GHG calculations for each country’s project. For each  country, 
“base case” (i.e., baseline) assumptions were provided in Annex 23  (GHG  
Calculation)  of the Funding Proposal.  
o We agree that TA to support implementation of credit lines is important. TA is  
envisaged  for every  project  supported by  the Facility.

(i.e. public  procurement,  credit  lines  and energy  efficiency  financing  facility)

o Adaptation to climate change: The AE will report on beneficiaries associated
